# War on terror

This article is about the international military campaign. For other uses, see [War on terror (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_on_terror_\(disambiguation\)).

The **war on terror**, officially the **Global War on Terrorism** (**GWOT**),[^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18] is a global military campaign initiated by the United States in response to the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) in 2001. A [global conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_conflict) spanning [multiple wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_conflicts_spanning_multiple_wars), some researchers and political scientists have argued that it replaced the [Cold War](325329.md).[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20]

The main targets of the campaign were [militant Islamist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Militant_Islamist) movements such as [al-Qaeda](1921.md), the [Taliban](30635.md), and their allies. Other major targets included the [Ba'athist regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba'ath_Party_\(Iraqi-dominated_faction\)) in [Iraq](26215470.md), which was deposed in an [invasion in 2003](201936.md), and various militant factions that fought during the [ensuing insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_insurgency_\(2003%E2%80%932011\)). Following its territorial expansion in 2014, the [Islamic State](9087364.md) also emerged as a key adversary of the United States. In 2026, the United States and [Israel](9282173.md) launched a [war on Iran](82533582.md), identifying it as the largest [state sponsor of terrorism](317645.md). Al-Qaeda's de facto leader, [Saif al-Adel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saif_al-Adel), is believed to be living in Iran.[^cite_note-21]

The term "war on terror" uses [war as a metaphor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_as_a_metaphor) to describe a variety of actions which fall outside the traditional definition of war. US president [George W. Bush](3414021.md) first [used the term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slogans_and_terms_derived_from_the_September_11_attacks) "war on terrorism" on 16 September 2001,[^cite_note-evilwar-22][^cite_note-evilwar2-23] and then "war on terror" a few days later in a formal speech to [Congress](31756.md).[^cite_note-whitehousejoint-24][^cite_note-cnntranscript-25] Bush indicated the enemy of the war on terror as "a radical network of terrorists and every government that supports them".[^cite_note-cnntranscript-25][^cite_note-26] The initial conflict was aimed at al-Qaeda, with the main theater in [Afghanistan](737.md) and [Pakistan](23235.md), a region that would later be referred to as "[AfPak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AfPak)".[^cite_note-27] The term "war on terror" was immediately criticized by individuals including [Richard Myers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Myers), then [chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff](364458.md), and eventually more nuanced terms came to be used by the [Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_W._Bush) to define the campaign.[^cite_note-Schmitt/slogan-28] While "war on terror" was never used as a formal designation of US operations,[^cite_note-Ambinder2013-29] a [Global War on Terrorism Service Medal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_War_on_Terrorism_Service_Medal) was and is issued by the [US Armed Forces](32212.md).

According to the [Costs of War Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costs_of_War_Project), the [post-9/11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-9/11) wars of the campaign have displaced 38 million people, the second largest number of [forced displacements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_displacements) of any conflict since 1900,[^cite_note-costs_displaced-30] and caused more than 4.5 million deaths (direct and indirect) in Afghanistan, Iraq, Libya, Pakistan, Somalia, Syria and Yemen. They also estimate that it has cost the [US Treasury](53667.md) over \$8 trillion.[^cite_note-costs2021-31][^cite_note-figures-32][^cite_note-Summary-33][^cite_note-washingtonpost20230515-11]

While support for the "war on terror" was high among the American public during its initial years, it had become deeply unpopular by the late 2000s.[^cite_note-Ackerman-34][^cite_note-Doherty,_Kiley-35] Controversy over the war has focused on its morality, casualties, and continuity, with critics questioning government measures that infringed civil liberties and human rights.[^cite_note-wired.com-36] Critics have notably described the [Patriot Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriot_Act) as "[Orwellian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orwellian)" due to its substantial expansion of the federal government's surveillance powers.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38] Controversial practices of [coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Security_Assistance_Force) [forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-National_Force_%E2%80%93_Iraq) have been condemned, including [drone warfare](38212231.md), surveillance, torture, [extraordinary rendition](1031191.md), and various war crimes.[^cite_note-Ball-39][^cite_note-theguardian.com-40][^cite_note-Drone_Warfare-41] The participating governments have been criticized for implementing authoritarian measures, repressing minorities,[^cite_note-Assimilation-42][^cite_note-911legacies.com-43] fomenting [Islamophobia](162173.md) globally,[^cite_note-reflection_journal-44] and causing negative impacts to health and environment.[^cite_note-merip.org-45][^cite_note-Administration-46][^cite_note-Ameem_Lutfi-47] Security analysts assert that there is no military solution to the conflict, pointing out that terrorism is not an identifiable enemy, and have emphasized the importance of negotiations and political solutions to resolve the underlying roots of the crises.[^cite_note-Richissin-48]

Starting in 2025, [Operation Southern Spear](81593523.md) emerged as an extension of the war on terror adapted to combat [narcoterrorism](221350.md) in Latin America, with the US government utilizing the same legal frameworks established in 2001 to justify the use of lethal force. Some analysts have identified this new approach as a hybridization of the war on terror and the [war on drugs](1181646.md).[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52]

## Etymology

The phrase *war on terror* was used to refer specifically to the military campaign led by the United States, the United Kingdom, and allied countries against organizations and regimes identified by them as terrorist, and usually excludes other independent [counter-terrorist operations and campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-terrorism) such as those by Russia and India. The conflict has also been referred to by other names, such as "[World War III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_III)",[^cite_note-53] "[World War IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_IV)" (assuming the [Cold War](325329.md) was World War III),[^cite_note-54] "Bush's War on Terror",[^cite_note-55] "The Long War",[^cite_note-56][^cite_note-WaPo-57] "The Forever War",[^cite_note-58] "The Global War on Terror",[^cite_note-59] "The War Against al-Qaeda",[^cite_note-60] or "The War of Terror".[^cite_note-61]

### Use of phrase and its development



[September 20, 2001, President Bush speaks to a joint session of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_2001_George_W._Bush_speech_to_a_joint_session_of_Congress)

&#x20;The phrase "war against terrorism" existed in North American popular culture and US political parlance before the war on terror.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63] But it was not until the 11 September attacks that it emerged as a globally recognizable phrase and part of everyday lexicon. [Tom Brokaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Brokaw), having just witnessed the collapse of one of the towers of the [World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Center_\(1973%E2%80%932001\)), declared "Terrorists have declared war on \[America]."[^cite_note-64] On 16 September 2001, at [Camp David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_David), US president George W. Bush used the phrase *war on terrorism* in an ostensibly unscripted comment when answering a journalist's question about the impact of enhanced law enforcement authority given to the [U.S. surveillance](40480495.md) agencies on Americans' civil liberties:&#x20;

> "This is a new kind of—a new kind of evil. And we understand. And the American people are beginning to understand. This *crusade*, this *war on terrorism* is going to take a while. And the American people must be patient. I'm going to be patient."[^cite_note-evilwar-22][^cite_note-evilwar2-23]

The reference to [Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades) became subject to heavy criticism due to its controversial connotations in the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world) and historical [Muslim-Christian relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim-Christian_relations).[^cite_note-CullCulbertWelch2003-65] On 20 September 2001, during a televised address to a joint session of Congress, George Bush said, "Our war on terror begins with al Qaeda, but it does not end there. It will not end until every terrorist group of global reach has been found, stopped and defeated."[^cite_note-whitehousejoint-24][^cite_note-cnntranscript-25] Both the term and the policies it denotes have been a source of ongoing controversy, as various critics and organizations like [Amnesty International](18947898.md) have argued that it has been used to justify unilateral [preventive war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preventive_war), human rights abuses and other violations of international law.[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67]

The political theorist Richard Jackson has argued that "the 'war on terrorism' \[...] is simultaneously a set of actual practices—wars, covert operations, agencies, and institutions—and an accompanying series of assumptions, beliefs, justifications, and narratives—it is an entire language or discourse".[^cite_note-68] Jackson cites, among many examples, a statement by [John Ashcroft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ashcroft) that "the attacks of September 11 drew a bright line of demarcation between the civil and the [savage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savage_\(pejorative_term\))".[^cite_note-69]

&#x20;Administration officials also described "terrorists" as hateful, treacherous, barbarous, mad, twisted, perverted, without faith, [parasitical](43937.md), inhuman, and, most commonly, evil.[^cite_note-70] Americans, in contrast, were described as brave, loving, generous, strong, resourceful, heroic, and respectful of human rights.[^cite_note-71] Denouncing the remarks of [George W. Bush](3414021.md), Osama Bin Laden stated during an interview on 21 October 2001:&#x20;

> "The events proved the extent of [terrorism](30636.md) that America exercises in the world. [Bush](3414021.md) stated that the world has to be divided in two: Bush and his supporters, and any country that doesn't get into the global crusade is with the terrorists. What terrorism is clearer than this? Many governments were forced to support this "new terrorism."[^cite_note-72]

### Decline of the phrase's usage by the US government

In April 2007, the British government announced publicly that it was abandoning the phrase "war on terror" because it found it less than helpful.[^cite_note-73] This was explained later by Lady [Eliza Manningham-Buller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eliza_Manningham-Buller). In her 2011 [Reith lecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reith_lecture), the former head of [MI5](100076.md) said that the 9/11 attacks were "a crime, not an act of war. So I never felt it helpful to refer to a war on terror."[^cite_note-74]



Letter from Barack Obama indicating appropriation of congressional funds for "Overseas Contingency Operations/Global War on Terrorism"

US president [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) rarely used the term, but in his inaugural address on 20 January 2009, he stated: "Our nation is at war, against a far-reaching network of violence and hatred."[^cite_note-75] In March 2009 the Defense Department officially changed the name of operations from "Global War on Terror" to "Overseas Contingency Operation" (OCO).[^cite_note-oco-wapost-76] In March 2009, the [Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama) requested that [Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon) staff members avoid the use of the term and instead to use "Overseas Contingency Operation".[^cite_note-oco-wapost-76] Basic objectives of the Bush administration "war on terror", such as targeting al Qaeda and building international counterterrorism alliances, remained in place.[^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78]

#### Abandonment of phrase

In May 2010, the Obama administration published a report outlining its [National Security Strategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Strategy_\(United_States\)). The document dropped the Bush-era phrase "global war on terror" and reference to "Islamic extremism," and stated, "This is not a global war against a tactic—terrorism, or a religion—Islam. We are at war with a specific network, al-Qaeda, and its terrorist affiliates who support efforts to attack the United States, our allies, and partners."[^cite_note-declover-79]

Usage of the term "war on terror" was initially discontinued in May 2010[^cite_note-declover-79] and again in May 2013.[^cite_note-whitehdefuniv-80] On 23 May 2013, US president [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) announced that the "war on terrorism" was over,[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-declover-79] saying that the US would not wage war against a tactic but would instead focus on a specific group of terrorist networks.[^cite_note-82][^cite_note-whitehdefuniv-80] Other American military campaigns during the 2010s have also been considered part of the "war on terror" by individuals and the media.[^cite_note-theguardian20150603-83] The rise of the Islamic State in Iraq and Syria during 2014–2015 led to the global [Operation Inherent Resolve](44120613.md), and an [international campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_military_intervention_against_the_Islamic_State) to destroy the terrorist organization. This was considered to be another campaign of the "war on terror".[^cite_note-theguardian20150603-83]

In December 2012, [Jeh Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeh_Johnson), the [General Counsel of the Department of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Counsel_of_the_Department_of_Defense), speaking at [Oxford University](31797.md), stated that the war against al-Qaeda would end when the terrorist group had been weakened so that it was no longer capable of "strategic attacks" and had been "effectively destroyed." At that point, the war would no longer be an armed conflict under [international law](8195726.md),[^cite_note-84] and the military fight could be replaced by a [law enforcement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_enforcement) operation.[^cite_note-85]

In May 2013, two years after [the killing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Osama_bin_Laden) of [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden), Barack Obama delivered a speech that employed the term *global war on terror* put in quotation marks (as officially transcribed by the White House): "Now, make no mistake, terrorists still threaten our nation. ... In Afghanistan, we will complete our transition to Afghan responsibility for that country's security. ... Beyond Afghanistan, we must define our effort not as a boundless 'global war on terror,' but rather as a series of persistent, targeted efforts to dismantle specific networks of violent extremists that threaten America. In many cases, this will involve partnerships with other countries." Nevertheless, in the same speech, in a bid to emphasize the legality of military actions undertaken by the US, noting that [Congress had authorised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authorization_for_Use_of_Military_Force_Against_Terrorists) the use of force, he went on to say, "Under domestic law, and international law, the United States is at war with al Qaeda, the Taliban, and their associated forces. We are at war with an organization that right now would kill as many Americans as they could if we did not stop them first. So this is a just war—a war waged proportionally, in last resort, and in self-defense."[^cite_note-whitehdefuniv-80][^cite_note-terrorover-86]

Nonetheless, the use of the phrase "war on terror" persists in US politics. In 2017, for example, US vice president [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence) called the 1983 Beirut barracks bombing "the opening salvo in a war that we have waged ever since—the global war on terror."[^cite_note-87]

## Background

### Precursor to 11 September attacks

See also: [Islamic terrorism](566821.md) and [List of Islamist terrorist attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Islamist_terrorist_attacks)

In May 1996, the group World Islamic Front for Jihad Against Jews and Crusaders (WIFJAJC), sponsored by [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden) (and later re-formed as [al-Qaeda](1921.md)),[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89] started forming a large base of operations in [Afghanistan](737.md), where the Islamist extremist regime of the [Taliban](30635.md) had seized power earlier in the year.[^cite_note-90] In August 1996, Bin Laden declared jihad against the United States.[^cite_note-91] In February 1998, Osama bin Laden signed a [fatwa](11259.md), as head of al-Qaeda, [declaring war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_war) on the West and Israel;[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93] in May al-Qaeda released a video declaring war on the US and the West.[^cite_note-94][^cite_note-95]

On 7 August 1998, al-Qaeda [struck the U.S. embassies in Kenya and Tanzania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_United_States_embassy_bombings), killing 224 people, including 12 Americans.[^cite_note-96] In retaliation, US president [Bill Clinton](3356.md) launched [Operation Infinite Reach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Infinite_Reach), a bombing campaign in [Sudan](27421.md) and Afghanistan against targets the US asserted were associated with WIFJAJC,[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98] although others have questioned whether a pharmaceutical plant in Sudan was used as a chemical warfare facility. The plant produced much of the region's [antimalarial drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antimalarial_drug)[^cite_note-newstatesman.com-99] and around 50% of Sudan's pharmaceutical needs.[^cite_note-100] The strikes failed to kill any leaders of WIFJAJC or the Taliban.[^cite_note-newstatesman.com-99]

Next came the [2000 millennium attack plots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_millennium_attack_plots), which included an attempted bombing of [Los Angeles International Airport](18131.md). On 12 October 2000, the [USS *Cole* bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Cole_bombing) occurred near the port of Yemen, and 17 [US Navy](20518076.md) sailors were killed.[^cite_note-101]

### 11 September attacks

Main article: [September 11 attacks](5058690.md)

On the morning of 11 September 2001, [nineteen men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hijackers_in_the_September_11_attacks) [hijacked](2075.md) four [jet airliners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_airliner), all of them bound for California. Once the hijackers assumed control of the jet airliners, they told the passengers that they had a [bomb](445224.md) on board and would spare the lives of passengers and crew once their demands were met—no passenger and crew actually suspected that they would use the jet airliners as [suicide weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_weapon) since it had never happened before in history; many previous hijacking attempts had been resolved with the passengers and crew escaping unharmed after obeying the hijackers.[^cite_note-Holmes-102][^cite_note-Keppel2008-103] The hijackers—members of al-Qaeda's [Hamburg cell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamburg_cell)[^cite_note-104]—intentionally crashed two jet airliners into the Twin Towers of the [World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Center_\(1973%E2%80%932001\)) in New York City. Both buildings collapsed within two hours from fire damage related to the crashes, destroying nearby buildings and damaging others. The hijackers crashed a third jet airliner into [the Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon) in [Arlington County, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington_County,_Virginia), just outside Washington, D.C. The fourth jet airliner crashed into a field near [Shanksville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanksville,_Pennsylvania), Pennsylvania, after some of its passengers and flight crew attempted to retake control of the jet airliner, which the hijackers had redirected toward Washington, D.C., to target the [White House](33057.md) or the [US Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Capitol). None of the flights had any survivors. A total of 2,977 victims and the 19 hijackers perished in the attacks.[^cite_note-105] Fifteen of the nineteen were citizens of [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), and the others were from the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) (2), [Egypt](8087628.md), and [Lebanon](17771.md).[^cite_note-cia.gov-106]

On 13 September, for the first time, [NATO](21133.md) invoked [Article 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_Article_5) of the [North Atlantic Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty), which commits each member state to consider an armed attack against one member state to be an armed attack against them all.[^cite_note-107] The invocation of Article 5 led to [Operation Eagle Assist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Eagle_Assist) and [Operation Active Endeavour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Active_Endeavour). On 18 September 2001, President Bush signed the [Authorization for Use of Military Force Against Terrorists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authorization_for_Use_of_Military_Force_of_2001), which Congress had passed a few days earlier; the authorization remains in effect and has been used to justify numerous military actions.

## American objectives



&#x20; [NATO](21133.md)

&#x20; [Trans-Sahara initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Saharan_Counterterrorism_Initiative)

&#x20; Major military operations (as of 2011) \
&#x20;     (**[Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_\(2001%E2%80%93present\)) • [Pakistan](12336420.md) • [Iraq](5043324.md) • [Somalia](3070332.md) • [Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_in_Yemen)**)

&#x20; Other allies involved in major operations

&#x20; Major terrorist attacks by al-Qaeda and affiliated groups: (as of 2011)&#x20;

***1**. [1998 United States embassy bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_United_States_embassy_bombings) • **2**. [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) • **3**. [2002 Bali bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Bali_bombings) • **4**. [2004 Madrid bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Madrid_train_bombings) • **5**. [2005 London bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_London_bombings) • **6**. [2008 Mumbai attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Mumbai_attacks)*

The [Authorization for Use of Military Force Against Terrorists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authorization_for_Use_of_Military_Force_Against_Terrorists) or "AUMF" was made law on 14 September 2001, to authorize the use of United States Armed Forces against those responsible for the 11 September attacks. It authorized the president to use all necessary and appropriate force against those nations, organizations, or persons he determines planned, authorized, committed, or aided the terrorist attacks that occurred on 11 September 2001, or harbored such organizations or persons, to prevent any future acts of international terrorism against the United States by such nations, organizations, or individuals. Congress declares this is intended to constitute specific statutory authorization within the meaning of section 5(b) of the [War Powers Resolution](209756.md) of 1973.

The [George W. Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_administration) defined the following objectives in the war on terror:[^cite_note-NatStratCombatTerror-108]

1. Defeat terrorists such as [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden), [Abu Musab al-Zarqawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Musab_al-Zarqawi) and destroy their organizations

2. Identify, locate, and demolish terrorists along with their organizations

3. Reject sponsorship, support, and sanctuary to terrorists&#x20;

   1. End the [state sponsorship of terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_sponsorship_of_terrorism)

   2. Establish and maintain an international standard of responsibility concerning combating terrorism

   3. Strengthen and maintain the international effort to combat terrorism

   4. Function with willing and able states

   5. Enable weak states

   6. Persuade reluctant states

   7. Compel unwilling states

   8. Intervene and dismantle material support for terrorists

   9. Abolish terrorist sanctuaries and havens

4. Reduce the underlying conditions that terrorists seek to exploit&#x20;

   1. Establish partnerships with the international community to strengthen weak states and prevent (re)emergence of terrorism

   2. Win the war of ideals

5. Protect US citizens and interests at home and abroad&#x20;

   1. Integrate the [National Strategy for Homeland Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Strategy_for_Homeland_Security)

   2. Attain domain awareness

   3. Enhance measures to ensure the integrity, reliability, and availability of critical, physical, and information-based infrastructures at home and abroad

   4. Implement measures to protect US citizens abroad

   5. Ensure an integrated incident management capacity

The 2001 AUMF has authorized the US president to launch military operations worldwide without congressional oversight or transparency. Between 2018 and 2020 alone, US forces initiated what it labelled "counter-terror" activities in 85 countries. Of these, the 2001 AUMF has been used to launch classified military campaigns in at least 22 countries.[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110] The 2001 AUMF has been widely perceived as a bill that grants the president powers to unilaterally wage perpetual "world wide wars".[^cite_note-111]

## Timeline

For a chronological guide, see [timeline of the War on Terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_War_on_Terror).

## Operation Enduring Freedom

Main article: [Operation Enduring Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Enduring_Freedom)



Campaign streamer awarded to units who have participated in [Operation Enduring Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_War_on_Terrorism_Expeditionary_Medal)

Operation Enduring Freedom is the official name used by the Bush administration for the [War in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_\(2001%E2%80%93present\)), together with three smaller military actions, under the umbrella of the global war on terror. [These global operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wars_and_battles_involving_al-Qaeda) are intended to seek out and destroy any al-Qaeda fighters or affiliates. Originally, the campaign was named "Eternal Justice" but due to widespread controversy and condemnation in the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world), the phrasing was changed to "Enduring Freedom".[^cite_note-CullCulbertWelch2003-65]

### Afghanistan

Main article: [War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)](19666611.md)

See also: [List of military operations in the war in Afghanistan (2001–2021)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_operations_in_the_war_in_Afghanistan_\(2001%E2%80%932021\))

On 20 September 2001, in the wake of the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md), George W. Bush delivered an ultimatum to the Taliban government of Afghanistan, the [Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan), to turn over Osama bin Laden and [al-Qaeda](1921.md) leaders operating in the country or face attack.[^cite_note-cnntranscript-25] The Taliban demanded evidence of bin Laden's link to 11 September attacks and, if such evidence warranted a trial, they offered to handle such a trial in an Islamic Court.[^cite_note-112]



U.S. Army soldier of the [10th Mountain Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/10th_Mountain_Division) in Nuristan Province, June 2007



An American soldier in Afghanistan's [Khost Province](457249.md)

Subsequently, in October 2001, US forces (with UK and coalition allies) [invaded Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Afghanistan) to oust the Taliban regime. On 7 October 2001, the official invasion began with British and US forces conducting [airstrike](323167.md) campaigns over enemy targets. Shortly after, Bush rejected a Taliban offer to hand over bin Laden on the condition the bombing campaign was halted,[^cite_note-113] and by mid-November, Kabul, the capital city of Afghanistan, [fell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Kabul_\(2001\)). The remaining al-Qaeda and Taliban remnants fell back to the rugged mountains of eastern Afghanistan, mainly Tora Bora. In December, Coalition forces (the US and its allies) [fought within that region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tora_Bora). It is believed that Osama bin Laden escaped to Pakistan during the battle.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115]

In March 2002, the US and other [NATO](21133.md) and non-NATO forces launched [Operation Anaconda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Anaconda), intending to destroy any remaining al-Qaeda and Taliban forces in the [Shah-i-Kot Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah-i-Kot_Valley) and [Arma Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arma_Mountains) of Afghanistan. The Taliban suffered heavy casualties and evacuated the region.[^cite_note-116]

The Taliban regrouped in western Pakistan and began to unleash an insurgent-style offensive against Coalition forces in late 2002.[^cite_note-117] Throughout southern and eastern Afghanistan, firefights broke out between the surging Taliban and Coalition forces. Coalition forces responded with a series of military offensives and an increase in troops in Afghanistan. In February 2010, Coalition forces launched [Operation Moshtarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Moshtarak) in southern Afghanistan along with other military offensives in the hopes that they would destroy the Taliban insurgency once and for all.[^cite_note-118] Peace talks were also underway between Taliban affiliated fighters and Coalition forces.[^cite_note-119]

In September 2014, Afghanistan and the United States signed a security agreement that allowed U.S. and NATO forces to remain in Afghanistan until at least 2024.[^cite_note-120] However, on 29 February 2020, the United States and the Taliban signed a conditional [peace deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_peace_process) in [Doha](26214389.md) which required that [US troops withdraw from Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_United_States_troops_from_Afghanistan_\(2011%E2%80%932016\)) within 14 months so long as the Taliban cooperated with the terms of the agreement not to "allow any of its members, other individuals or groups, including Al Qaeda, to use the soil of Afghanistan to threaten the security of the United States and its allies".[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-proposedwithdrawal-122] The Afghan government was not a party to the deal and rejected its terms regarding release of prisoners.[^cite_note-nprreject-123] After [Joe Biden](145422.md) became president, he moved back the target withdrawal date to 31 August 2021.[^cite_note-\:9-124] On 15 August 2021, the Afghan capital [Kabul fell](68481047.md) to a surprisingly effective [Taliban offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive), ending the [war in Afghanistan](19666611.md). The US military and NATO troops took control of Kabul's [Hamid Karzai International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul_International_Airport) for use in [Operation Allies Refuge](68466548.md) and the [large-scale evacuation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_evacuation_from_Afghanistan) of foreign citizens and certain vulnerable [Afghans](2235.md), executed in cooperation with the Taliban.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129]

On 30 August 2021, the United States completed its hasty [withdrawal of its military from Afghanistan](66883576.md). The withdrawal was heavily criticized both domestically and abroad for being chaotic and haphazard,[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131] as well as for lending more momentum to the [Taliban offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive).[^cite_note-132] However, many European countries followed suit, including Britain, Germany, Italy, and Poland.[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134] Despite evacuating over 120,000 people, the [large-scale evacuation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Kabul_airlift) has also been criticized for leaving behind hundreds of American citizens, residents, and family members.[^cite_note-135]

#### International Security Assistance Force

Main article: [International Security Assistance Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Security_Assistance_Force)



[US Secretary of Defense](43998.md) [Donald Rumsfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Rumsfeld) with [Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff](364458.md) General [Richard B. Myers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Myers) and joined by military representatives from [29 countries of the worldwide coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Security_Assistance_Force) on the war against terrorism, at [The Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon), 11 March 2002



Map of countries contributing troops to ISAF as of 5 March 2010. Major contributors (over 1000 troops) in dark green, other contributors in light green, and former contributors in magenta.

The NATO-led [International Security Assistance Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Security_Assistance_Force) (ISAF) was created in December 2001 to assist the [Afghan Transitional Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Transitional_Administration) and the first post-Taliban elected government. With a renewed Taliban insurgency, it was announced in 2006 that ISAF would replace the US troops in the province as part of Operation Enduring Freedom.

The British [16th Air Assault Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_Air_Assault_Brigade) (later reinforced by [Royal Marines](93985.md)) formed the core of the force in southern Afghanistan, along with troops and helicopters from Australia, Canada, and the Netherlands. The initial force consisted of roughly 3,300 British, 2,000 Canadian, 1,400 Dutch, and 240 Australian troops, along with special forces from Denmark and [Estonia](28222445.md) and small contingents from other nations. The monthly supply of cargo containers through Pakistani route to ISAF in Afghanistan is over 4,000 costing around 12 billion in Pakistani Rupees.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140]

### Philippines

Main article: [Operation Enduring Freedom – Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Enduring_Freedom_%E2%80%93_Philippines)



[US Special Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Special_Forces) soldier and infantrymen of the [Philippine Army](6032559.md)

In January 2002, the [United States Special Operations Command, Pacific](45204749.md) deployed to the [Philippines](23440.md) to advise and assist the [Armed Forces of the Philippines](23448.md) in combating Filipino Islamist groups.[^cite_note-141] The operations were mainly focused on removing the [Abu Sayyaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Sayyaf) group and [Jemaah Islamiyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jemaah_Islamiyah) (JI) from their stronghold on the island of [Basilan](297003.md).[^cite_note-142] The second portion of the operation was conducted as a humanitarian program called "Operation Smiles". The goal of the program was to provide medical care and services to the Basilan region as part of a "Hearts and Minds" initiative.[^cite_note-143]

Joint Special Operations Task Force – Philippines disbanded in June 2014,[^cite_note-144] ending a successful 12-year mission.[^cite_note-145] After JSOTF-P had disbanded, as late as November 2014, American forces continued to operate in the Philippines under the name "PACOM Augmentation Team", until 24 February 2015.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] On 1 September 2017, US Secretary of Defense [Jim Mattis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Mattis) designated [Operation Pacific Eagle – Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Pacific_Eagle_%E2%80%93_Philippines) (OPE-P) as a contingency operation to support the Philippine government and the military in their efforts to isolate, degrade, and defeat the affiliates of ISIL (collectively referred to as ISIL-Philippines or ISIL-P) and other terrorist organisations in the Philippines.[^cite_note-148] By 2018, American operations within the Philippines against terrorist groups involved as many as 300 advisers.[^cite_note-149]

### Trans-Sahara (Northern Africa)

Main article: [Operation Juniper Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Juniper_Shield)



A map of the conflict in northern Mali

Operation Enduring Freedom – Trans Sahara (OEF-TS), now Operation Juniper Shield, is the name of the military operation conducted by the US and partner nations in the Sahara/Sahel region of Africa, consisting of counter-terrorism efforts and policing of arms and drug trafficking across central Africa.

The [conflict in northern Mali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Mali_conflict_\(2012%E2%80%93present\)) began in January 2012 with radical Islamists (affiliated to al-Qaeda) advancing into northern Mali. The Malian government had difficulty maintaining full control over the country. The fledgling government requested support from the international community in combating the Islamic militants. In January 2013, France intervened at the request of the Malian government and deployed troops to the region. They launched [Operation Serval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Serval) on 11 January 2013, with the hopes of dislodging the al-Qaeda affiliated groups from northern Mali.[^cite_note-150]

### Horn of Africa and the Red Sea

Main article: [Operation Enduring Freedom – Horn of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Enduring_Freedom_%E2%80%93_Horn_of_Africa)

Further information: [Ethiopian occupation of Somalia (2006–2009)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Somalia_\(2006%E2%80%932009\)), [Somali Civil War (2009–present)](21454663.md), [United States intervention in Somalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_intervention_in_Somalia), and [CIA activities in Somalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_activities_in_Somalia)

Operation Enduring Freedom – Horn of Africa is an extension of Operation Enduring Freedom. Unlike other operations within Operation Enduring Freedom, OEF-HOA does not have a specific organization as its target. OEF-HOA instead focuses its efforts to disrupt and detect militant activities in the region and to work with willing governments to prevent the reemergence of militant cells and activities.[^cite_note-151]

In October 2002, the [Combined Joint Task Force – Horn of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_Joint_Task_Force_%E2%80%93_Horn_of_Africa) (CJTF-HOA) was established in [Djibouti](17207794.md) at [Camp Lemonnier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Lemonnier).[^cite_note-152] It contains approximately 2,000 personnel including US military and special operations forces (SOF) and coalition force members, [Combined Task Force 150](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_Task_Force_150) (CTF-150).

Task Force 150 consists of ships from a shifting group of nations, including Australia, Canada, France, Germany, Italy, the Netherlands, Pakistan, New Zealand, and the United Kingdom. The primary goal of the coalition forces is to monitor, inspect, board, and stop suspected shipments from entering the Horn of Africa region and affecting the United States' [Operation Iraqi Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Iraqi_Freedom).

Included in the operation is the training of selected armed forces units from Djibouti, [Kenya](188171.md), and [Ethiopia](187749.md) in counter-terrorism and [counter-insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-insurgency) tactics. Humanitarian efforts conducted by CJTF-HOA include rebuilding schools and medical clinics and providing medical services to countries whose forces are being trained.

The program expands as part of the [Trans-Saharan Counterterrorism Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Saharan_Counterterrorism_Initiative), with CJTF personnel also assisting in training the armed forces of [Chad](5488.md), Niger, [Mauritania](18778516.md), and [Mali](19127.md). However, the war on terror does not include Sudan, where over 400,000 have died in an ongoing civil war.

On 1 July 2006, a Web-posted message purportedly written by Osama bin Laden urged [Somalis](1571696.md) to build an [Islamic state](2646438.md) in the country; warned Western governments that the al-Qaeda network would fight against them if they intervened there.[^cite_note-BIN-LADEN-MESSAGE-IRAQ-SOMALIA-153]

The Prime Minister of Somalia claimed that three "terror suspects" from the [1998 United States embassy bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_United_States_embassy_bombings) are being sheltered in Kismayo.[^cite_note-154] On 30 December 2006, al-Qaeda deputy leader [Ayman al-Zawahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayman_al-Zawahiri) called upon Muslims worldwide to fight against Ethiopia and the TFG in Somalia.[^cite_note-155]

On 8 January 2007, the US launched the [Battle of Ras Kamboni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Battle_of_Ras_Kamboni) by bombing [Ras Kamboni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ras_Kamboni) using [AC-130](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AC-130) gunships.[^cite_note-156]

On 14 September 2009, [US Special Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Forces_\(United_States_Army\)) killed two men and wounded and captured two others near the Somali village of [Baarawe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baraawe_raid). Witnesses claim that helicopters used for the operation launched from French-flagged warships, but that could not be confirmed. A Somali-based al-Qaida-affiliated group, the [Al-Shabaab](8726463.md), has verified the death of "sheik commander" [Saleh Ali Saleh Nabhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saleh_Ali_Saleh_Nabhan) along with an unspecified number of militants.[^cite_note-157] Nabhan, a Kenyan, was wanted in connection with the [2002 Mombasa attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Mombasa_attacks).[^cite_note-158]

## Military operations by country

### Iraq War

Main article: [Iraq War](5043324.md)

Further information: [Iraq War and the war on terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_and_the_war_on_terror)

#### 2002 State of the Union Address

Main article: [Axis of evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_of_evil)

&#x20;During his [2002 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_State_of_the_Union_Address), [George W. Bush](3414021.md) accused [North Korea](21255.md), [Iran](14653.md), and Iraq of propping up [state-sponsored terrorism](317645.md) and pursuing Weapons of Mass Destruction. These countries were portrayed as a global threat and categorised under the terminology referred to as "[Axis of evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_of_evil)".[^cite_note-159] Regarding [Iraq](7515928.md), Bush alleged:&#x20;

> "Iraq continues to flaunt its hostility toward America and to support terror. The Iraqi regime has plotted to develop anthrax and nerve gas and nuclear weapons for over a decade. This is a regime that has already used poison gas to murder thousands of its own citizens, leaving the bodies of mothers huddled over their dead children. This is a regime that agreed to international inspections, then kicked out the inspectors. This is a regime that has something to hide from the civilized world."[^cite_note-160]

#### Prelude

Further information: [Preparations for the 2003 invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preparations_for_the_2003_invasion_of_Iraq) and [Saddam Hussein and al-Qaeda link allegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein_and_al-Qaeda_link_allegations)

In October 2002, [United States Congress](31756.md) passed the "[Authorization for Use of Military Force Against Iraq Resolution of 2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authorization_for_Use_of_Military_Force_Against_Iraq_Resolution_of_2002)", which empowered the US president to order military attack against Iraq.[^cite_note-ResolutionText-161][^cite_note-162] On 5 February 2003, Secretary of State [Colin Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Powell) made a [presentation before the UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_and_the_Iraq_War#Colin_Powell's_presentation) which claimed to implicate Iraq in building a secret WMD program and having ties to [Al-Qaeda](1921.md).[^cite_note-163]

&#x20;On 17 March 2003, Bush issued an ultimatum to [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) and his two sons to flee Iraq within a 48-hour deadline or else face "military conflict".[^cite_note-whitehouse20230317-164] Justifying his policy Bush declared:&#x20;

> "Terrorists and terror states do not reveal these threats with fair notice, in formal declarations—and responding to such enemies only after they have struck first is not self-defense, it is suicide. The security of the world requires disarming Saddam Hussein now."[^cite_note-whitehouse20230317-164]

#### Invasion of Iraq

Main article: [2003 invasion of Iraq](201936.md)



A British [C-130J Hercules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-130J_Hercules) aircraft launches [flare countermeasures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flare_\(countermeasure\)) before being the first coalition aircraft to land on the newly reopened military runway at [Baghdad International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baghdad_International_Airport).

The Iraq War began in March 2003 with an air campaign, which was immediately followed by a [US-led ground invasion](201936.md).[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-Anghie2005-166] The Bush administration cited UNSC Resolution 1441, which warned of "serious consequences" for violations such as [Iraq possessing weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction). The Bush administration also [stated the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_and_US_Global_War_on_Terror) was part of the war on terror, a claim later [questioned and contested](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_war_on_terror). Iraq had been listed as a [state sponsor of terrorism](317645.md) by the US since 1990,[^cite_note-167] when [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) [invaded Kuwait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Kuwait).

The first ground attack came at the [Battle of Umm Qasr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Umm_Qasr) on 21 March 2003, when a combined force of British, US and Polish forces seized control of the port city of [Umm Qasr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Qasr).[^cite_note-168] [Baghdad](4492.md), Iraq's capital city, [fell to US troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Baghdad_\(2003\)) in April 2003 and Saddam Hussein's government quickly dissolved.[^cite_note-169] On 1 May 2003, [Bush announced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Mission_Accomplished_speech) that major combat operations in Iraq had ended.[^cite_note-170]

#### Iraqi Insurgency (2003–11)

Main article: [Iraqi Insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_insurgency_\(2003%E2%80%932011\))

However, an insurgency arose against the US-led coalition and the newly developing Iraqi military and post-Saddam government. The rebellion, which included [al-Qaeda-affiliated groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_Iraq), led to many coalition casualties. Other elements of the insurgency were led by fugitive members of President Hussein's [Ba'ath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba'ath) regime, which included Iraqi nationalists and [pan-Arabists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabist). Many insurgency leaders were [Islamists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamists) and claimed to be fighting a religious war to reestablish the Islamic [Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate) of centuries past.[^cite_note-171] Saddam Hussein [was captured by US forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Red_Dawn) in December 2003 and [was executed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Execution_of_Saddam_Hussein) in 2006.

In 2004, the insurgent forces grew stronger. The US launched offensives on insurgent strongholds in cities like [Najaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Najaf_\(2004\)) and [Fallujah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Battle_of_Fallujah).

In January 2007, President Bush presented a new strategy for Operation Iraqi Freedom based upon counterinsurgency theories and tactics developed by General [David Petraeus](3223944.md). The [Iraq War troop surge of 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_troop_surge_of_2007) was part of this "new way forward", which, along with US backing of [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) groups it had previously sought to defeat, has been credited with a widely recognized dramatic decrease in violence by up to 80%.[^cite_note-172]

The war entered a new phase on 1 September 2010,[^cite_note-173] with the official end of U.S. combat operations.

#### War in Iraq (2013–17)

Main article: [War in Iraq (2013–2017)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Iraq_\(2013%E2%80%932017\))

President Obama ordered the withdrawal of most troops in 2011, but began redeploying forces in 2014 to fight the [Islamic State](9087364.md) group.[^cite_note-174] As of July 2021, there were approximately 2,500 US troops in Iraq, who continue to assist in the mission to combat the remnants of IS.[^cite_note-175]

### Pakistan

Main articles: [Pakistan in the war on terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_in_the_war_on_terror), [Insurgency in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa](12336420.md), and [Pakistan and state-sponsored terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_and_state-sponsored_terrorism)

See also: [American drone strikes in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_drone_strikes_in_Pakistan), [List of American drone strikes in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_American_drone_strikes_in_Pakistan), [Killing of Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Osama_bin_Laden), and [Pakistan–United States relations](7144235.md)

Following the 11 September attacks, former president of Pakistan [Pervez Musharraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pervez_Musharraf) sided with the US against the Taliban government in Afghanistan after an ultimatum by then US president George W. Bush. Musharraf agreed to give the US the use of three airbases for Operation Enduring Freedom. [United States secretary of state](32293.md) [Colin Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Powell) and other US administration officials met with Musharraf. On 19 September 2001, Musharraf addressed the people of Pakistan and stated that, while he opposed military tactics against the Taliban, Pakistan risked being endangered by an alliance of India and the US if it did not cooperate. In 2006, Musharraf testified that this stance was pressured by threats from the US, and revealed in his memoirs that he had "war-gamed" the United States as an adversary and decided that it would end in a loss for Pakistan.[^cite_note-176]

On 12 January 2002, Musharraf gave a speech against Islamic extremism. He unequivocally condemned all acts of terrorism and pledged to combat Islamic extremism and lawlessness within Pakistan itself. He stated that his government was committed to rooting out extremism and made it clear that the banned militant organizations would not be allowed to resurface under any new name. He said, "the recent decision to ban extremist groups promoting militancy was taken in the national interest after thorough consultations. It was not taken under any foreign influence".[^cite_note-177]

In 2002, the Musharraf-led government took a firm stand against the jihadi organizations and groups promoting extremism, and arrested [Maulana Masood Azhar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maulana_Masood_Azhar), head of the [Jaish-e-Mohammed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaish-e-Mohammed), and [Hafiz Muhammad Saeed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafiz_Muhammad_Saeed), chief of the [Lashkar-e-Taiba](649718.md), and took dozens of activists into custody. An official ban was imposed on the groups on 12 January.[^cite_note-178] Later that year, the [Saudi](349303.md) born [Zayn al-Abidn Muhammed Hasayn Abu Zubaydah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Zubaydah) was arrested by Pakistani officials during a series of joint US–Pakistan raids. Zubaydah is said to have been a high-ranking al-Qaeda official with the title of operations chief and in charge of running al-Qaeda training camps.[^cite_note-179] Other prominent al-Qaeda members were arrested in the following two years, namely [Ramzi bin al-Shibh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramzi_bin_al-Shibh), who is known to have been a financial backer of al-Qaeda operations, and [Khalid Sheikh Mohammed](190884.md), who at the time of his capture was the third highest-ranking official in al-Qaeda and had been directly in charge of the planning for 11 September attacks.

In 2004, the [Pakistan Army](1965925.md) launched a campaign in the [Federally Administered Tribal Areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federally_Administered_Tribal_Areas) of Pakistan's [Waziristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waziristan) region, sending in 80,000 troops. The goal of the conflict was to remove the al-Qaeda and Taliban forces in the area.

After the fall of the Taliban regime, many members of the Taliban resistance fled to the Northern border region of Afghanistan and Pakistan, where the Pakistani army had previously had little control. With the logistics and air support of the United States, the Pakistani Army captured or killed numerous al-Qaeda operatives such as Khalid Sheikh Mohammed, wanted for his involvement in the USS *Cole* bombing, the [Bojinka plot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bojinka_plot), and the killing of *[Wall Street Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street_Journal)* reporter [Daniel Pearl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Pearl).

The United States has carried out a campaign of [drone attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drone_attacks_in_Pakistan) on targets all over the Federally Administered Tribal Areas. However, the [Pakistani Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehrik-i-Taliban_Pakistan) still operates there. To this day, it is estimated that 15 US soldiers have been killed while fighting al-Qaeda and Taliban remnants in Pakistan since the war on terror began.[^cite_note-180]

Osama bin Laden, his wife, and son, were all killed on 2 May 2011, during a raid conducted by the [United States special operations forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_special_operations_forces) in [Abbottabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbottabad), Pakistan.[^cite_note-181]

The use of drones by the Central Intelligence Agency in Pakistan to carry out operations associated with the global war on terror sparks debate over sovereignty and the laws of war. The US Government uses the CIA rather than the [US Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Air_Force) for strikes in Pakistan to avoid breaching sovereignty through military invasion. The United States was criticized by a report on drone warfare and [aerial sovereignty](353557.md) for abusing the term 'global war on terror' to carry out military operations through government agencies without formally declaring war.

After the 11 September attacks, US economic and security aid to Pakistan spiked considerably. With the authorization of the Enhanced Partnership for Pakistan Act, Pakistan was granted US\$7.5 billion over five years from FY2010–FY2014.[^cite_note-182]

### Yemen

Main articles: [Al-Qaeda insurgency in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_insurgency_in_Yemen), [United States intervention in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_intervention_in_Yemen), and [Yemeni civil war (2014–present)](46215853.md)

|  | This section needs to be **updated**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(March 2025)* |
| --- | --- |

The United States has conducted a [series of military strikes on al-Qaeda militants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemeni_al-Qaeda_crackdown) in Yemen since the war on terror began.[^cite_note-183] Yemen has a weak central government and a powerful tribal system that leaves large lawless areas open for militant training and operations. [Al-Qaeda](1921.md) has a strong presence in the country.[^cite_note-184] On 31 March 2011, AQAP declared the Al-Qaeda Emirate in Yemen after its captured most of [Abyan Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abyan_Governorate).[^cite_note-185]

The US, in an effort to support Yemeni counter-terrorism efforts, increased their military aid package to Yemen from less than \$11 million in 2006 to more than \$70 million in 2009, as well as providing up to \$121 million for development over the next three years.[^cite_note-186]

## Current phase: Islamic State, Iran, and focus on narcoterrorism (2021–present)

Main articles: [United States intervention in Syria](43683196.md), [2025–2026 United States airstrikes in Syria](81885282.md), [2025 United States strikes in Nigeria](83205369.md), [War on drugs](1181646.md), and [Operation Southern Spear](81593523.md)

During the 2020s, the war on terror has decreased in volume and intensity following the closure of the wars in Iraq ([2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_United_States_troops_from_Iraq_\(2007%E2%80%932011\)), with residual operations until [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_United_States_troops_from_Iraq_\(2020%E2%80%932021\))) and Afghanistan (complete withdrawal in [2021](66883576.md)). This shift represents a transition from large-scale military interventions—with hundreds of thousands of troops, ground invasions, and prolonged occupations—to a more surgical, [hybrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_warfare), and sustainable approach centered on intelligence, selective strikes, and regional alliances.[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192] For their part, the US and [NATO](21133.md) prioritize cyber defense, online counter-narratives, and cooperation with allies, which reduces media "noise" and their own casualties.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] On July 31, 2022, [Ayman al-Zawahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayman_al-Zawahiri), [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden)'s key successor, was killed in a US drone strike in Kabul, Afghanistan, marking the end of decades of centralized leadership in al-Qaeda.[^cite_note-195] As of 2025, various global operations in the campaign are ongoing, including a [US military intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_military_intervention_in_Somalia_\(2007%E2%80%93present\)) in [Somalia](27358.md).[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197] Meanwhile, in 2025, [airstrikes continue in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_airstrikes_on_Yemen).[^cite_note-198]

Meanwhile, [ISIS–K](48029263.md) remains a structural terrorist cell due to its decentralized and transnational operational capacity, evidenced by a series of high-impact attacks—including the January 2024 suicide bombings in Kerman, Iran (94 deaths),[^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200] the March 2024 assault on Crocus City Hall in Moscow (more than 140 fatalities),[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202] the foiled August 2024 plot against Taylor Swift concerts in Vienna,[^cite_note-203] and the January 1, 2025 vehicle-and-explosives attack in New Orleans (at least 15 dead)[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205]—which demonstrate its ability to project lethal violence far beyond its core bases in Afghanistan and Pakistan by exploiting security vacuums created by the 2021 US withdrawal and the instability in Syria following the December 2024 collapse of the Assad regime.[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] This resilience is sustained by highly sophisticated digital propaganda that has revitalized recruitment efforts among Central Asian diasporas (especially Tajiks) in Russia, Türkiye, Europe, and the United States via encrypted platforms;[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209] and by robust logistical and financial networks that enable the mobilization of battle-hardened fighters (estimated at 4,000–6,000 in Afghanistan and more than 2,500 active operatives in Syria and Iraq according to 2025 UN and CENTCOM reports).[^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211]

In 2024, the [proxy war between Iran and Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Israel_proxy_conflict) escalated to a [series of direct confrontations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Iran%E2%80%93Israel_conflict) between the two countries, and in June 2025, they fought a [12-day war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War),[^cite_note-bbc.com-212] [involving the United States](80262844.md).[^cite_note-Sanger21June2025-213][^cite_note-Fordo21June2025-214]

In 2025, the US' military escalation against alleged [narcoterrorists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narcoterrorist) linked to [Venezuela](32374.md)—such as the [Cartel of the Suns](45272133.md), the [Tren de Aragua](70859925.md) and the [National Liberation Army](822032.md)—reframed Latin American narcoterrorism as a global threat, which in turn opened the door to a possible invasion of Venezuelan territory. The designation of the Cartel of the Suns as a Foreign Terrorist Organization, along with the deployment of the USS Gerald R. Ford near Venezuelan waters and a series of strikes against vessels labeled as narco-trafficker assets, represented a turning point in the region: the shift was no longer about interdiction, but about counterterrorism within the Americas. Likewise, [Operation Southern Spear](81593523.md) has raised new questions about whether a second war on terror has begun.[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221] By adopting the same legal and rhetorical framework used during the post-9/11 war on terror,[^cite_note-222] treating traffickers as “unlawful combatants” and justifying lethal force even in international waters, Washington opened the door to broader military operations—including targeted strikes on criminal-controlled areas, direct intervention, and even potential ground actions if the threat were deemed systemic. This strategic realignment could pull other regional actors into a collective security framework, legitimize multilateral military cooperation, and reshape hemispheric security under an anti-terror paradigm, extending far beyond traditional anti-drug efforts.[^cite_note-223]

On December 15, 2025, the US carried out Operation Hawkeye Strike against the [Islamic State](9087364.md) following the [Palmyra attack](81852348.md).

On 25 December 2025, US president [Donald Trump](4848272.md) authorized strikes against [Islamic State](9087364.md) militants in northwest [Nigeria](21383.md).[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226]

Beginning in late January 2026, the [United States](3434750.md) carried out its largest military buildup in the Middle East since the [2003 invasion of Iraq](201936.md), deploying air, naval, and missile defense assets amid [escalating tensions](82533582.md) with [Iran](14653.md), which the United States has attributed to [Iran's nuclear program](721807.md), the [2025–2026 Iranian protests](81976180.md), and a [government crackdown that killed thousands of demonstrators](82036668.md).[^cite_note-Cancian-227][^cite_note-228] On 28 February 2026, this culminated in a [series of joint military strikes](82546969.md) in Iran by the United States and [Israel](9282173.md).[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231]

## Other military operations

### Operation Inherent Resolve (Syria and Iraq)

Main articles: [Operation Inherent Resolve](44120613.md), [War against the Islamic State](43517689.md), and [List of wars and battles involving the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wars_and_battles_involving_the_Islamic_State)

Further information: [US-led intervention in Iraq (2014–2021)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US-led_intervention_in_Iraq_\(2014%E2%80%932021\)), [Spillover of the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spillover_of_the_Syrian_civil_war), and [United States intervention in Syria](43683196.md)



US soldiers in Syria during [Operation Inherent Resolve](44120613.md)

|  | This section needs to be **updated**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(February 2025)* |
| --- | --- |

The Obama administration began to re-engage in Iraq with a series of airstrikes aimed at ISIL starting on 10 August 2014.[^cite_note-232] On 9 September 2014, President Obama said that he had the authority he needed to take action to destroy the militant group known as the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](9087364.md), citing the 2001 Authorization for the Use of Military Force Against Terrorists, and thus did not require additional approval from Congress.[^cite_note-233] The following day on 10 September 2014 President Barack Obama made a televised speech about ISIL, which he stated: "Our objective is clear: We will degrade, and ultimately destroy, ISIL through a comprehensive and sustained counter-terrorism strategy".[^cite_note-ObamaSpeech10SEP14-234] Obama has authorized the deployment of additional US Forces into Iraq, as well as authorizing direct military operations against ISIL within Syria.[^cite_note-ObamaSpeech10SEP14-234] On the night of 21/22 September the United States, Saudi Arabia, Bahrain, the UAE, Jordan and Qatar started air attacks against ISIL in Syria.[^cite_note-235]

In October 2014, it was reported that the US Department of Defense considered military operations against ISIL to be under Operation Enduring Freedom for campaign medal awarding.[^cite_note-236] On 15 October, the military intervention became known as "Operation Inherent Resolve".[^cite_note-237]

### Islamic State of Lanao and the Battle of Marawi

With the rise of the [Islamic State in Iraq and the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_in_Iraq_and_the_Levant) (ISIL), jihadist offshoots sprang up in [regions around the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISIL_territorial_claims), including the Philippines. The [Maute group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maute_group), composed of former [Moro Islamic Liberation Front](51673.md) guerrillas and foreign fighters led by [Omar Maute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Maute), the alleged founder of a *Dawlah Islamiya*, declared loyalty to ISIL and began clashing with Philippine security forces and staging [bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Davao_City_bombing). On 23 May 2017, the group attacked the city of [Marawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marawi), resulting in the bloody [Battle of Marawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Marawi) that lasted 5 months. After the decisive battle, remnants of the group were reportedly still recruiting in 2017 and 2018.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239]

### Libyan War

Main articles: [Factional violence in Libya (2011–2014)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factional_violence_in_Libya_\(2011%E2%80%932014\)), [Libyan civil war (2014–2020)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_civil_war_\(2014%E2%80%932020\)), and [United States intervention in Libya (2015–2019)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_intervention_in_Libya_\(2015%E2%80%932019\))



An [AV-8B Harrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_AV-8B_Harrier_II) takes off from the flight deck of [USS *Wasp*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Wasp_\(LHD-1\)) during [Operation Odyssey Lightning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_intervention_against_ISIL#Libya), 8 August 2016.

NBC News reported that in mid-2014, [ISIL had about 1,000 fighters in Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq_and_the_Levant_in_Libya). Taking advantage of a power vacuum in the center of the country, far from the major cities of [Tripoli](31232.md) and [Benghazi](204114.md), ISIL expanded rapidly over the next 18 months. Local militants were joined by jihadists from the rest of North Africa, the Middle East, Europe, and the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus). The force absorbed or defeated other Islamist groups inside [Libya](17633.md) and the central ISIL leadership in [Raqqa](858965.md), [Syria](7515849.md), began urging foreign recruits to head for Libya instead of Syria. [ISIL](9087364.md) seized control of the coastal city of [Sirte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirte) in early 2015 and then began to expand to the east and south. By the beginning of 2016, it had effective control of 120 to 150 miles of coastline and portions of the interior and had reached Eastern Libya's major population center, Benghazi. In spring 2016, [AFRICOM](9347800.md) estimated that ISIL had about 5,000 fighters in its stronghold of Sirte.[^cite_note-Did_Obama_Defeat_ISIS_in_Libya-240]

However, the indigenous rebel groups who had staked their claims to Libya and turned their weapons on ISIL, with the help of airstrikes by Western forces, including US drones, the Libyan population resented the outsiders who wanted to establish a fundamentalist regime on their soil. Militias loyal to the new [Libyan unity government](17633.md), plus a separate and rival force loyal to a former officer in the [Gaddafi regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaddafi_regime), [launched an assault on ISIL outposts in Sirte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sirte_\(2016\)) and the surrounding areas that lasted for months. According to US military estimates, ISIL's ranks shrank to somewhere between a few hundred and 2,000 fighters. In August 2016, the [US military began airstrikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_intervention_against_ISIL#Libya) that, along with continued pressure on the ground from the Libyan militias, pushed the remaining ISIL fighters back into Sirte. In all, US drones and planes hit ISIL nearly 590 times, and the Libyan militias reclaimed the city in mid-December.[^cite_note-Did_Obama_Defeat_ISIS_in_Libya-240] On 18 January 2017, ABC News reported that two USAF [B-2 bombers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_Grumman_B-2_Spirit) struck two ISIL camps 28 miles (45 km) south of Sirte, the airstrikes targeted between 80 and 100 ISIL fighters in multiple camps, a crewless aircraft also participated in the airstrikes. NBC News reported that as many as 90 ISIL fighters were killed in the strike, a [US defense](7279897.md) official said that "This was the largest remaining [ISIL presence in Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_in_Libya)," and that "They have been largely marginalized, but I am hesitant to say they have been eliminated in Libya."[^cite_note-Did_Obama_Defeat_ISIS_in_Libya-240]

### American military intervention in Cameroon

In October 2015, the US began deploying 300 soldiers[^cite_note-241] to [Cameroon](5447.md), with the invitation of the Cameroonian government, to support African forces in a non-combat role in their fight against ISIL insurgency in that country. The troops' primary missions will focus on providing intelligence support to local forces and conducting reconnaissance flights.[^cite_note-242]

### Operation Active Endeavour

Main article: [Operation Active Endeavour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Active_Endeavour)

Operation Active Endeavour was a [naval operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navy) of NATO started in October 2001 in response to the 11 September attacks. It operated in the Mediterranean and was designed to prevent the movement of militants or [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_of_mass_destruction) and to enhance the security of shipping in general.[^cite_note-243]

### Fighting in Kashmir

Main article: [Kashmir conflict](3030955.md)



**Political map:** the Kashmir region districts

In a 'Letter to American People' written by [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden) in 2002, he stated that one of the reasons he was fighting America is because of its support of India on the Kashmir issue.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-CFRKashmir-245] Indian sources claimed that in 2006, al-Qaeda claimed they had established a wing in Kashmir; this worried the Indian government.[^cite_note-246] India also argued that al-Qaeda has strong ties with the Kashmir militant groups [Lashkar-e-Taiba](649718.md) and [Jaish-e-Mohammed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaish-e-Mohammed) in Pakistan.[^cite_note-247] While on a visit to Pakistan in January 2010, US Defense Secretary [Robert Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Gates) stated that al-Qaeda was seeking to destabilize the region and planning to provoke a nuclear war between India and Pakistan.[^cite_note-248]

In September 2009, a US [drone strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drone_attacks_in_Pakistan) reportedly killed [Ilyas Kashmiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilyas_Kashmiri_\(militant\)), who was the chief of [Harkat-ul-Jihad al-Islami](5592225.md), a Kashmiri militant group associated with al-Qaeda.[^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250] Kashmiri was described by [Bruce Riedel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Riedel) as a 'prominent' al-Qaeda member,[^cite_note-251] while others described him as the head of military operations for al-Qaeda.[^cite_note-252] [Waziristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waziristan) had now become the new battlefield for Kashmiri militants, who were now fighting [NATO](21133.md) in support of al-Qaeda.[^cite_note-253] On 8 July 2012, Al-Badar Mujahideen, a breakaway faction of the Kashmir-centric terror group Hizbul Mujahideen, on the conclusion of their two-day Shuhada Conference, called for a mobilization of resources for the continuation of jihad in Kashmir.[^cite_note-254] In June 2021, an air force station in Jammu (in India-administered Kashmir) was attacked by a drone. Investigators were uncertain whether a state or non-state actor initiated the attack.[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256]

### Anti-terror campaigns by other powers

As part of the [Iran–Israel proxy conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Israel_proxy_conflict) and the [Iran–Saudi Arabia proxy conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_proxy_conflict), both [Israel](9282173.md) and [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) has engaged in its own war on terror against [Iranian](14653.md)-backed insurgent groups, and against [Al-Qaeda in the Arabian Peninsula (AQAP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Arabian_Peninsula) and [Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_%E2%80%93_Yemen_Province) in Saudi Arabia's case.[^cite_note-257] Although Israel did not directly participate in the [2003 invasion of Iraq](201936.md), it reportedly urged the US not to delay an attack on [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein).[^cite_note-258] Before the US-led invasion of Iraq in 2003, Israeli intelligence reportedly provided information to Washington.[^cite_note-259] Israel launched a 34-day military conflict against [Hezbollah](13919.md) in [Lebanon, northern Israel and the Golan Heights during mid-2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Lebanon_War).[^cite_note-260] In Saudi Arabia's case, its own war on terror has been heightened by [its intervention in the Yemeni civil war](46230181.md) since March 26, 2015. Both Al-Qaeda in the Arabian Peninsula (AQAP) and Islamic State had a presence in [Yemen](350939.md) before the Saudi-led intervention.[^cite_note-261]

[Colombia](5222.md) has also engaged on its own war on terror,[^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263] as terrorism by both [guerrillas](13266029.md) and [paramilitaries](2873402.md) remains a major concern in the country since [the escalation of armed violence in the 2000s](1857628.md#2000%E2%80%932006) in the context of the [Colombian conflict](1857628.md).[^cite_note-264] [Álvaro Uribe](243680.md)'s presidency, in particular, was marked by a significant focus on counter-terrorism and counterinsurgency. The counterterrorism measures of [Plan Colombia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plan_Colombia) acquired further expansion during the presidency of [George W. Bush](3414021.md) and an important focus on national security after the events of [9/11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9/11), as the threat of global terrorism received greater attention.[^cite_note-state-265]

In the 2010s, the [People's Republic of China](5405.md) has also been engaged in its own war on terror, predominantly a domestic campaign in response to violent actions by [Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_people) separatist movements in the [Xinjiang conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_conflict).[^cite_note-266] This campaign was widely criticized in international media due to the perception that it unfairly targets and persecutes [Chinese Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Muslims),[^cite_note-267] potentially resulting in a negative backlash from China's predominantly [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) Uighur population. [CCP general secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) [Xi Jinping](2017814.md)'s [regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_secretaryship_of_Xi_Jinping) has imprisoned up to two million [Uyghurs](31783.md) and other [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_China) ethnic minorities in [Xinjiang internment camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_internment_camps), where they are reportedly [subject to abuse and torture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Human_Rights_Office_report_on_Xinjiang).[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269]

Russia has also been engaged on its own, also largely internally focused, counter-terrorism campaign often termed a war on terror, during the [Second Chechen War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Chechen_War), the [Insurgency in the North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurgency_in_the_North_Caucasus), and the [Russian military intervention in the Syrian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_military_intervention_in_the_Syrian_Civil_War).[^cite_note-270] Like China's war on terror, Russia has also been focused on [separatist](286527.md) and [Islamist](15012.md) movements that use [political violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence) to achieve their ends.[^cite_note-271]

#### Nepali Civil War

In the 1990s, a Maoist rebellion broke out in Nepal against the monarchy starting the [Nepali civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_civil_war).
