Western responses to the rise of a powerful Marxist movement in South Asia that controlled significant parts of the Nepali countryside utilised the language of the Global War on Terror. America thereby allocated millions of dollars in aid and supplied thousands of machine guns and military advisers to Nepal's government, supplied thousands of machine guns and other weaponry, and provided military advisers and training for the RNA, while British foreign office minister [Mike O'Brien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_O'Brien_\(British_politician\)) stated that "Nepal’s struggle against the insurgents should be seen as part of the wider ‘war against terrorism.’"[^cite_note-272] While George Bush also coached American support to the Nepali government in the context of a wider front in the GWOT.[^cite_note-273]

## International military support

Main articles: [Participants in Operation Enduring Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Participants_in_Operation_Enduring_Freedom) and [Multi-National Force – Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-National_Force_%E2%80%93_Iraq)

See also: [Coalition combat operations in Afghanistan in 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_combat_operations_in_Afghanistan_in_2008) and [War in Afghanistan order of battle, 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_order_of_battle,_2012)



The United Kingdom was the second-largest contributor of troops in [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_\(2001%E2%80%93present\)).

The invasion of Afghanistan is seen to have been the first action of this war, and initially involved forces from the United States, the United Kingdom, and the Afghan [Northern Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Alliance). Since the initial invasion period, these forces were augmented by troops and aircraft from Australia, Canada, Denmark, France, Italy, the Netherlands, New Zealand, and Norway, among others. In 2006, there were about 33,000 troops in Afghanistan.

On 12 September 2001, less than 24 hours after the 11 September attacks in New York City and Washington, D.C., NATO invoked Article 5 of the [North Atlantic Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty). It declared the attacks to be an attack against all 19 NATO member countries. Australian Prime Minister [John Howard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Howard) also stated that Australia would invoke the [ANZUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ANZUS) Treaty along similar lines.[^cite_note-274]

In the following months, NATO took a broad range of measures to respond to the threat of terrorism. On 22 November 2002, the member states of the [Euro-Atlantic Partnership Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro-Atlantic_Partnership_Council) (EAPC) decided on a Partnership Action Plan against Terrorism, which explicitly states, "\[The] EAPC States are committed to the protection and promotion of fundamental freedoms and human rights, as well as the rule of law, in combating terrorism."[^cite_note-275] NATO started naval operations in the Mediterranean Sea designed to prevent the movement of terrorists or weapons of mass destruction as well as to enhance the security of shipping in general called Operation Active Endeavour.

Support for the US cooled when America made clear its determination to invade Iraq in late 2002. Still, many of the "coalition of the willing" countries that unconditionally supported the US-led military action have sent troops to Afghanistan, particularly neighboring Pakistan, which has disowned its earlier support for the Taliban and contributed tens of thousands of soldiers to the conflict. Pakistan was also engaged in the [Insurgency in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa](12336420.md) (a.k.a. Waziristan War or North-West Pakistan War). Supported by US intelligence, Pakistan attempted to remove the Taliban insurgency and al-Qaeda element from the northern tribal areas.[^cite_note-276]

## Domestic aspects

Main article: [Patriot Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriot_Act)

Further information: [Mass surveillance in the United States](40480495.md) and [NSPM-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSPM-7)



A [US Immigration and Customs Enforcement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Immigration_and_Customs_Enforcement) helicopter patrols the airspace over New York City.

In addition to military efforts abroad, in the [aftermath of 9/11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_9/11), the Bush Administration increased domestic efforts to prevent future attacks. Various government bureaucracies that handled security and military functions were reorganized. A new cabinet-level agency called the [United States Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) was created in November 2002 to lead and coordinate the largest reorganization of the US federal government since the consolidation of the armed forces into the Department of Defense.[^cite_note-277]

The Justice Department launched the [National Security Entry-Exit Registration System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Entry-Exit_Registration_System) for certain male non-citizens in the US, requiring them to register in person at offices of the [Immigration and Naturalization Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_and_Naturalization_Service).

The USA PATRIOT Act of October 2001 dramatically reduces restrictions on law enforcement agencies' ability to search telephone, e-mail communications, medical, financial, and other records; eases restrictions on foreign intelligence gathering within the United States; expands the [Secretary of the Treasury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_the_Treasury)'s authority to regulate financial transactions, particularly those involving foreign individuals and entities; and broadens the discretion of law enforcement and immigration authorities in detaining and deporting immigrants suspected of terrorism-related acts. The act also expanded the definition of terrorism to include domestic terrorism, thus enlarging the number of activities to which the USA PATRIOT Act's expanded law enforcement powers could be applied. A new [Terrorist Finance Tracking Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorist_Finance_Tracking_Program) monitored the movements of terrorists' financial resources (discontinued after being revealed by *[The New York Times](30680.md)*). Global telecommunication usage, including those with no links to terrorism,[^cite_note-278] is being collected and monitored through the [NSA electronic surveillance program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSA_electronic_surveillance_program). The Patriot Act is still in effect.

Political interest groups have stated that these laws remove important restrictions on governmental authority and constitute a dangerous encroachment on civil liberties, possibly [unconstitutional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutionality) violations of the [Fourth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution). On 30 July 2003, the [American Civil Liberties Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_Liberties_Union) (ACLU) filed the first legal challenge against Section 215 of the Patriot Act, claiming that it allows the [FBI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI) to violate a citizen's [First Amendment](31653.md) rights, Fourth Amendment rights, and right to [due process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Due_process), by granting the government the right to search a person's business, bookstore, and library records in a terrorist investigation, without disclosing to the individual that records were being searched.[^cite_note-279] Also, governing bodies in many communities have passed symbolic resolutions against the act.



[John Walker Lindh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Walker_Lindh) was captured as an enemy combatant during the United States' [2001 invasion of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_\(2001%E2%80%93present\)).

In a speech on 9 June 2005, Bush said that the USA PATRIOT Act had been used to bring charges against more than 400 suspects, more than half of whom had been convicted. Meanwhile, the ACLU quoted Justice Department figures showing that 7,000 people have complained of abuse of the Act.[^cite_note-280]

The Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency ([DARPA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DARPA)) launched an initiative in early 2002 by creating the [Total Information Awareness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_Information_Awareness) program, designed to promote information technologies for counterterrorism. This program, facing criticism, has since been defunded by Congress.[^cite_note-281]

By 2003, 12 major conventions and protocols were designed to combat terrorism. These were adopted and ratified by many states. These conventions require states to cooperate on principal issues regarding unlawful seizure of aircraft, the physical protection of nuclear materials, and the freezing of assets of militant networks.[^cite_note-282]

In 2005, the UN Security Council adopted [Resolution 1624](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1624) concerning incitement to commit acts of terrorism and the obligations of countries to comply with international human rights laws.[^cite_note-283] Although both resolutions require mandatory annual reports on counter-terrorism activities by adopting nations, the United States and Israel have both declined to submit reports. In the same year, the [United States Department of Defense](7279897.md) and the [Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff](364458.md) issued a planning document, by the name "National Military Strategic Plan for the War on Terrorism", which stated that it constituted the "comprehensive military plan to prosecute the Global War on Terror for the [Armed Forces of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_of_the_United_States)...including the findings and recommendations of the [9/11 Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9/11_Commission) and a rigorous examination with the Department of Defense".

On 9 January 2007, the House of Representatives passed a bill, by a vote of 299–128, enacting many of the recommendations of the 9/11 Commission. The bill passed in the US Senate,[^cite_note-284] by a vote of 60–38, on 13 March 2007. It was signed into law on 3 August 2007 by President Bush. It became Public Law 110–53. In July 2012, the US Senate passed a resolution urging that the [Haqqani Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haqqani_Network) be designated a [foreign terrorist organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_State_Department_list_of_Foreign_Terrorist_Organizations).[^cite_note-285]

The [Office of Strategic Influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Strategic_Influence) was secretly created after 9/11 to coordinate propaganda efforts, but was closed soon after it was discovered. The Bush administration implemented the [Continuity of Operations Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continuity_of_Operations_Plan) (also known as Continuity of Government) to ensure that the US government could continue operations in catastrophic circumstances.

## Extrajudicial detention

### "Extraordinary rendition"



Alleged "extraordinary rendition" illegal flights of the [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA), as reported by *[Rzeczpospolita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rzeczpospolita_\(newspaper\))*

[^cite_note-286]



CIA's Extraordinary Rendition and Detention Program – countries involved in the Program, according to the 2013 [Open Society Foundations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_Society_Foundations)' report on [torture](47702.md)

[^cite_note-287]

[^cite_note-288]

After 11 September attacks, the United States government commenced a program of illegal "[extraordinary rendition](1031191.md)", sometimes referred to as "irregular rendition" or "forced rendition", the government-sponsored [abduction](169376.md) and [extrajudicial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial) transfer of a person from one country to transferee countries, with the consent of transferee countries.[^cite_note-54_countries-289][^cite_note-290][^cite_note-theguardian.com-40] The aim of extraordinary rendition is often conducting [torture](47702.md) on the detainee that would be difficult to conduct in the US legal environment, a practice known as [torture by proxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torture_by_proxy). Starting in 2002, the US government rendered hundreds of [illegal combatants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_combatant) for US detention, and transported detainees to US-controlled sites as part of an extensive interrogation program that included [torture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enhanced_interrogation).[^cite_note-291] Extraordinary rendition continued under the [Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) administration, with targets being interrogated and subsequently taken to the US for trial.[^cite_note-292]

The United Nations considers one nation abducting the citizens of another a [crime against humanity](61099.md).[^cite_note-MyUser_The_Washington_Post_July_26_2015c-293] In July 2014 the [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights) condemned the [government of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Poland) for participating in CIA extraordinary rendition, ordering Poland to pay restitution to men who had been abducted, taken to a CIA [black site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_site) in Poland, and tortured.[^cite_note-European_Court_of_Human_Rights-294][^cite_note-295][^cite_note-MyUser_Reuters.com_February_24_2015c-296]

### Rendition to "Black Sites"

See also: [CIA black sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_black_sites) and [Ghost detainee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghost_detainee)

In 2005, *The Washington Post* and [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) (HRW) published revelations concerning the kidnapping of detainees by the [US Central Intelligence Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Central_Intelligence_Agency) and their transport to "[black sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_site)", covert prisons operated by the CIA whose existence is denied by the US government. The [European Parliament](9581.md) published a report connecting the use of such secret detention Black Sites for detainees kidnapped as part of extraordinary rendition (*See the [European Parliament's investigation and report](1031191.md#European_Parliament's_investigation_and_report)*). Although some Black Sites have been known to exist inside European Union states, these detention centers violate the [European Convention on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Convention_on_Human_Rights) (ECHR) and the [UN Convention Against Torture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Convention_Against_Torture), treaties that all EU member states are bound to follow.[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299] The US had ratified the United Nations Convention Against Torture in 1994.[^cite_note-300]

According to [ABC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_News_\(United_States\)), two such facilities, in countries mentioned by Human Rights Watch, have been closed following the recent publicity with the CIA relocating the detainees. Almost all of these detainees were tortured as part of the "[enhanced interrogation techniques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enhanced_interrogation_techniques)" of the CIA.[^cite_note-AbcNews051205-301] Despite the closure of these sites, their legacies in certain countries continue to live on and haunt domestic politics.[^cite_note-302]

#### Criticism of the US media's withholding of coverage

Major American newspapers, such as *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*, have been criticized for deliberately withholding publication of articles reporting locations of Black Sites. The Post defended its decision to suppress this news on the ground that such revelations "could open the [US government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_government) to legal challenges, particularly in foreign courts, and increase the risk of political condemnation at home and abroad." However, according to [Fairness and Accuracy in Reporting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairness_and_Accuracy_in_Reporting) "the possibility that illegal, unpopular government actions might be disrupted is not a consequence to be feared, however—it's the whole point of the [US First Amendment](31653.md). ... Without the basic fact of where these prisons are, it's difficult if not impossible for 'legal challenges' or 'political condemnation' to force them to close." FAIR argued that the damage done to the global reputation of the United States by the continued existence of black-site prisons was more dangerous than any threat caused by the exposure of their locations.[^cite_note-FAIR_2005-303]

> The complex at Stare Kiejkuty, a Soviet-era compound once used by German intelligence in World War II, is best known as having been the only Russian intelligence training school to operate outside the Soviet Union. Its prominence in the Soviet era suggests that it may have been the facility first identified—but never named—when the *Washington Post*'s Dana Priest revealed the existence of the CIA's secret prison network in November 2005.[^cite_note-304]

The journalists who exposed this provided their sources, and this information and documents were provided to *The Washington Post* in 2005. In addition, they also identified that Black Sites are concealed:

> Former European and US intelligence officials indicate that the secret prisons across the European Union, first identified by the Washington Post, are likely not permanent locations, making them difficult to identify and locate. What some believe was a network of secret prisons was most probably a series of facilities used temporarily by the United States when needed, officials say. Interim "black sites"—secret facilities used for covert activities—can be as small as a room in a government building, which only becomes a black site when a prisoner is brought in for short-term detainment and interrogation.

The journalists went on to explain that "Such a site, sources say, would have to be near an airport." The airport in question is the [Szczytno-Szymany International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Szczytno-Szymany_International_Airport).

In response to these allegations, former Polish intelligence chief, [Zbigniew Siemiatkowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zbigniew_Siemiatkowski), embarked on a media blitz and claimed that the allegations were "... part of the domestic political battle in the US over who is to succeed current Republican president George W Bush," according to the German news agency Deutsche Presse Agentur.[^cite_note-305]

#### Prison ships

The United States has also been accused of operating "[floating prisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prison_ship)" to house and transport those arrested in its war on terror, according to human rights lawyers. They have claimed that the US has tried to conceal the numbers and whereabouts of detainees. Although no credible information to support these assertions has ever come to light, the alleged justification for prison ships is primarily to remove the ability for jihadists to target a fixed location to facilitate the escape of high value targets, commanders, operations chiefs etc.[^cite_note-306]

### Guantanamo Bay detention camp



Detainees upon arrival at [Camp X-Ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_X-Ray_\(Guantanamo\)), January 2002

The US government set up the [Guantanamo Bay detention camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guantanamo_Bay_detention_camp) in 2002, a United States [military prison](1505096.md) located in [Guantanamo Bay Naval Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guantanamo_Bay_Naval_Base).[^cite_note-Independent060429-307] President [Bush](3414021.md) declared that the [Geneva Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Convention), which protects [prisoners of war](25008.md), would not apply to Taliban and al-Qaeda detainees captured in Afghanistan.[^cite_note-308] Since inmates were [detained indefinitely without trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indefinite_detention_without_trial) and several detainees have allegedly been [tortured](47702.md), this camp is considered to be a major breach of human rights by [Amnesty International](18947898.md).[^cite_note-withouttrial-309] The detention camp was set up by the US government on Guantanamo Bay since the military base is not legally domestic US territory and thus was a "legal black hole."[^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311] Most prisoners of Guantanamo were eventually freed without ever being charged with any crime, and were transferred to other countries.[^cite_note-312] By July 2021, 40 men remain in the prison and almost three-quarters of them have never been criminally charged. They're known as "forever prisoners" and are being detained indefinitely.[^cite_note-313] As of 2025, 15 detainees remain, with three awaiting transfer, nine who have been charged or convicted of [war crimes](48626.md), and three who are held in indefinite [law-of-war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_war) detention without facing tribunal charges nor being recommended for release.

## Major terrorist attacks and plots since 9/11

Following the launch of the "war on terror" by the United States, several Islamist militant groups, as well as militant individuals, have launched attacks against the assets of the US-led coalition, including in Western countries where active warfare is not taking place.

### Attacks by Al-Qaeda

Main articles: [al-Qaeda](1921.md) and [Timeline of al-Qaeda attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_al-Qaeda_attacks)

- The [2002 Bali bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Bali_bombings) in Indonesia were committed by various members of [Jemaah Islamiyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jemaah_Islamiyah), an organization linked to Al-Qaeda.

- [Al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Islamic_Maghreb) claimed responsibility for the [11 April 2007 Algiers bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/11_April_2007_Algiers_bombings), which targeted the office of Algerian Prime Minister and a police station.

- Morocco blamed Al-Qaeda for the [2011 Marrakech bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Marrakech_bombing) which targeted French nationals. However, al-Qaeda denies involvement in the attack.

- To date, no one has claimed responsibility for the 2012 [US Consulate attack in Benghazi](37018641.md) in Libya, and [Al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Islamic_Maghreb), pro-al-Qaeda militias, and individuals "sympathetic to al-Qaeda" are considered to be the orchestrators of the attack. The attacks were launched 18 hours after al-Qaeda Emir [Ayman al-Zawahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayman_al-Zawahiri) released a video urging Muslims to attack American targets in Libya to avenge the killing of al-Qaeda leader [Abu Yahya al-Libi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Yahya_al-Libi). The release of the video, as well as the launching of the attacks, coincided with the 11th anniversary of the 9/11 attacks.

- The gunmen in the [*Charlie Hebdo* shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Hebdo_shooting) in Paris identified themselves as belonging to al-Qaeda's [Al-Qaeda in the Arabian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Arabian_Peninsula).

- [Al-Qaeda in the Arabian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Arabian_Peninsula) claimed responsibility for the [Naval Air Station Pensacola shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Air_Station_Pensacola_shooting) in the United States.

### Islamic State

Main articles: [Islamic State](9087364.md) and [List of terrorist incidents linked to the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_terrorist_incidents_linked_to_the_Islamic_State)

See also: [List of wars and battles involving the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wars_and_battles_involving_the_Islamic_State)

- 2013 [Reyhanlı bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Reyhanl%C4%B1_car_bombings) in Turkey that led to 52 deaths and the injury of 140 people.

- 2014 [Canadian parliament shootings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_shootings_at_Parliament_Hill,_Ottawa), an ISIL-inspired attack on Canada's Parliament, resulting in the death of a Canadian soldier and that of the perpetrator.

- 2015 [Porte de Vincennes siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porte_de_Vincennes_siege) perpetrated by [Amedy Coulibaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amedy_Coulibaly) in Paris, which led to four deaths and the injury of nine others.

- [2015 Corinthia Hotel attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Corinthia_Hotel_attack) on 27 January in Libya that resulted in 10 deaths.

- [2015 Sana'a mosque bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_2015_Sanaa_mosque_bombings) on 20 March that led to the death of 142 and injury of 351 people.

  [^cite_note-314]

- 2015 [Curtis Culwell Center attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curtis_Culwell_Center_attack) on 3 May 2015 that resulted in the injury of one [security officer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Security_officer).

- [November 2015 Paris attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2015_Paris_attacks) on the 13th, which left at least 137 dead and injured at least 352 civilians, caused France to be put under a state of emergency, close its borders, and deploy three French contingency plans.

  [^cite_note-315]

  &#x20;Islamic State claimed responsibility for the attacks,

  [^cite_note-316]

  &#x20;with French president [François Hollande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Hollande) later stated the attacks were carried out "by the Islamic state with internal help".

  [^cite_note-317]

- [2015 San Bernardino attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_San_Bernardino_attack) on 2 December 2015, two gunmen attacked a county building in San Bernardino, California, killing 16 people and injuring 24 others.

  [^cite_note-318]

- [2016 Brussels bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Brussels_bombings) on 22 March 2016 two bombing attacks, first at [Brussels Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Airport) and the second at the [Maalbeek/Maelbeek metro station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maalbeek/Maelbeek_metro_station), killed 35 people and injured more than 300.

  [^cite_note-319]

- 2016 [Orlando nightclub shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando_nightclub_shooting) on 12 June 2016, a gunman opened fire at the Pulse nightclub in Orlando, Florida, killing 50 people and wounding 53 others. It was the second-worst mass shooting in U.S. history.

  [^cite_note-320]

- 2024 [Crocus City Hall attack](76418995.md), [ISIS–K](48029263.md) gunmen launched an [attack on a concert hall](76418995.md) in [Krasnogorsk](2399024.md), Russia, killing 145 people and wounding 551 others. It was the deadliest terrorist attack on Russian soil since the [Beslan school siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beslan_school_siege) in 2004.

### Attacks by other Islamist militant groups and individuals

- The [2003 Casablanca bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Casablanca_bombings) were carried out by [Salafia Jihadia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafia_Jihadia) militant group.

- After the [2003 Istanbul bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Istanbul_bombings), which attacked British and Jewish targets, Turkey charged 74 people with involvement, including Syrian al-Qaeda member [Loai al-Saqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loai_al-Saqa). Islamist group [Great Eastern Islamic Raiders' Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Eastern_Islamic_Raiders'_Front) claimed responsibility for the attacks

- The [2004 Madrid train bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Madrid_train_bombings) in Spain were carried out by Moroccan-born [Jamal Zougam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamal_Zougam) and five other individuals in opposition to Spanish participation in the [2003 US-led invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_American_invasion_of_Iraq).

- The [7 July 2005 London bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7_July_2005_London_bombings) in the United Kingdom, which targeting [London's public transport system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_London), were perpetrated by four Islamist militants.

- The [2009 Fort Hood shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Fort_Hood_shooting) in the United States was committed by [Nidal Hasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nidal_Hasan), who had been in communication with [Anwar al-Awlaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anwar_al-Awlaki). The [US Department of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Department_of_Defense) classified the shooting as an incident of [workplace violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workplace_violence).

- The [2007 Glasgow International Airport attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Glasgow_International_Airport_attack) in the United Kingdom was carried out by [Bilal Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilal_Abdullah) and [Kafeel Ahmed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafeel_Ahmed).

- The 2012 [Toulouse and Montauban shootings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toulouse_and_Montauban_shootings), which targeted French soldiers and a Jewish school, were committed by [Mohammed Merah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Merah). Although Merah claimed ties to al-Qaeda, French authorities have denied any connection.

- The 2023 [7th October Attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Hamas-led_attack_on_Israel), which targeted Israeli soldiers and civilians, was committed by [Hamas](13913.md) and its [allies](75192468.md). With a [barrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Palestinian_rocket_attacks_on_Israel_in_2023) of at least 3,000 rockets launched against Israel and vehicle-transported and [powered paraglider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Powered_paragliding) and infantry incursions into Israel, it was the deadliest terrorist attack in [Israel's history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Israel) and the third-deadliest terrorist attack in the 21st century.

  [^cite_note-321]

## Casualties

There is no widely agreed-upon figure for the number of people who have been killed so far in the war on terror, as the Bush Administration has defined it to include the war in Afghanistan, the war in Iraq, and operations elsewhere. According to [Joshua Goldstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua_S._Goldstein), an [international relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_relations) professor at the [American University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_University), the global war on terror has seen fewer war deaths than any other decade in the past century.[^cite_note-322]

A 2015 report by the [International Physicians for the Prevention of Nuclear War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Physicians_for_the_Prevention_of_Nuclear_War) and the Physicians for Social Responsibility and Physicians for Global Survival estimated between 1.3 million to 2 million casualties from the war on terror.[^cite_note-323] A report from September 2021 by Brown University's [Watson Institute for International and Public Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watson_Institute_for_International_and_Public_Affairs) "Costs of War" project puts the total number of casualties of the war on terror in Iraq, Afghanistan and Pakistan at between 518,000 and 549,000. This number increases to between 897,000 and 929,000 when the wars in Syria, Yemen, and other countries are included. The report estimated that many more may have died from indirect effects of war, such as water loss and disease.[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-costs2021-31] They also estimated that over 38 million people have been displaced by the post-9/11 wars participated in by the United States in [Afghanistan](737.md), Pakistan, Iraq, Libya, Syria, [Yemen](350939.md), Somalia, and the [Philippines](23440.md); 26.7 million people have returned home following displacement.[^cite_note-costs_displaced-30][^cite_note-figures-32] The conflict has caused the largest number of [forced displacements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_displacements) by any single war since 1900, except [World War II](32927.md).[^cite_note-costs_displaced-30]

In a 2023 report, the "Costs of War" project estimated that, as a result of the destruction of infrastructure, economies, public services, and the environment, there have been between 3.6 and 3.7 million indirect deaths in the post-9/11 war zones, with the total death toll being 4.5 to 4.6 million and rising.[^cite_note-325] The report defined post-9/11 war zones as conflicts that included significant United States counter-terrorism operations since 9/11, which in addition to the wars in [Iraq](55579888.md), [Afghanistan](19666611.md) and [Pakistan](12336420.md), also includes the civil wars in [Syria](30741795.md), [Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemeni_Civil_War_\(2014%E2%80%93present\)), [Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Crisis_\(2011%E2%80%93present\)) and [Somalia](3070332.md). The report derived its estimate of indirect deaths using a calculation from the [Geneva Declaration of Secretariat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Declaration_on_Armed_Violence_and_Development), which estimates that for every person directly killed by war, four more die from the indirect consequences of war. The report's author Stephanie Savell stated that in an ideal scenario, the preferable way of quantifying the total death toll would have been by studying excess mortality, or by using on-the-ground researchers in the affected countries.[^cite_note-washingtonpost20230515-11]

An estimated 7,052 US military combatants, over 8,100 US military contractors, and more than 14,800 US-allied coalition troops are estimated to have been killed in the wars as of 2023.[^cite_note-costs2021-31]

The total number of insurgent deaths since the commencement of the war on terror in 2001 is generally estimated as being well into the hundreds of thousands, with hundreds of thousands of others captured or arrested. Some estimates for regional conflicts include the following:

### Iraq

Main article: [Casualties of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualties_of_the_Iraq_War)

Footage of leaked [Apache gunship strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/12_July_2007_Baghdad_airstrike) in Baghdad, July 2007

In Iraq, some 26,544 insurgents were killed by the American-led coalition and the Iraqi Security Forces from 2003 to 2011.[^cite_note-326] 119,752 suspected insurgents were arrested in Iraq from 2003 to 2007 alone, at which point 18,832 suspected insurgents had been reported killed;[^cite_note-327] applying this same arrested-to-captured ratio to the total number of insurgents killed would equate to approximately 26,500 insurgents killed and 168,000 arrested from 2003 to 2011. At least 4,000 foreign fighters ([generally estimated at 10–20% of the insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_insurgency_\(2003%E2%80%932011\)#Foreign_participants) at that point) had been killed by September 2006, according to an official statement from [al-Qaeda in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_Iraq).[^cite_note-328] Insurgent casualties in the [2011–2013 phase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_insurgency_\(2011%E2%80%932013\)) of the Iraqi conflict numbered 916 killed, with 3,504 more arrested.

From 2014 to the end of 2017, the United States government stated that over 80,000 Islamic State insurgents had been killed by American and allied airstrikes from 2014 to the end of 2017, in both Iraq and Syria. The majority of these strikes occurred within Iraq.[^cite_note-329] ISIL deaths caused by the Iraqi Security Forces during this time are uncertain, but were probably significant. Over 26,000 ISF members were killed fighting ISIL from 2013 to the end of 2017,[^cite_note-330] with ISIL losses likely being of a similar scale.

Total casualties in Iraq range from 62,570 to 1,124,000:

- [Iraq Body Count project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Body_Count_project) documented 187,499 to 211,046 civilian dead from 2003 to 2020 (the status of 24,000 people as civilians or combatants was not clear), with over 300,000 violent deaths including combatants in total.

- 110,600 deaths in total according to the [Associated Press](18935732.md) from March 2003 to April 2009.

  [^cite_note-331]

- 151,000 deaths in total according to the [Iraq Family Health Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Family_Health_Survey).

- [Opinion Research Business (ORB) poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ORB_survey_of_Iraq_War_casualties) conducted 12–19 August 2007 estimated 1,033,000 violent deaths due to the Iraq War. The range given was 946,000 to 1,120,000 deaths. A nationally representative sample of approximately 2,000 Iraqi adults answered whether any members of their household (living under their roof) were killed due to the Iraq War. 22% of the respondents had lost one or more household members. ORB reported that "48% died from a gunshot wound, 20% from the impact of a car bomb, 9% from aerial bombardment, 6% as a result of an accident, and 6% from another blast/ordnance."<

  [^cite_note-ORB-333]

  [^cite_note-ORB2-334]

  [^cite_note-ORB3-335]

- A minimum of 62,570 civilian deaths reported in the mass media up to 28 April 2007 according to [Iraq Body Count project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Body_Count_project).

- 4,431 [US Department of Defense](7279897.md) dead (941 non-hostile deaths), and 31,994 wounded in action during Operation Iraqi Freedom. 74 US Military Dead (36 non-hostile deaths), and 298 wounded in action during Operation New Dawn as of 4 May 2020

### Afghanistan

Main articles: [Coalition casualties in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_casualties_in_Afghanistan) and [Civilian casualties in the war in Afghanistan (2001–2021)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civilian_casualties_in_the_war_in_Afghanistan_\(2001%E2%80%932021\))

Insurgent and terrorist deaths in Afghanistan are hard to estimate. [Afghan Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Taliban) losses are most likely of a similar scale to Afghan National Army and Police losses; that is, around 62,000 from 2001 to the end of 2018.[^cite_note-338] In addition, al-Qaeda's main branch and ISIL's Afghanistan branch are each thought to have lost several thousand killed there since 2001.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340]

Total casualties in Afghanistan range from 10,960 to 249,000:[^cite_note-Casualties-341]

- 16,725–19,013 civilians killed according to [Cost of War project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Friends_Service_Committee#Cost_of_War_project) from 2001 to 2013

  [^cite_note-342]

- According to Marc W. Herold's extensive database,

  [^cite_note-343]

  &#x20;between 3,100 and 3,600 civilians were directly killed by US [Operation Enduring Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Enduring_Freedom) bombing and Special Forces attacks between 7 October 2001 and 3 June 2003. This estimate counts only "impact deaths"—deaths that occurred in the immediate aftermath of an explosion or shooting—and does not count deaths that occurred later as a result of injuries sustained, or deaths that occurred as an indirect consequence of the US airstrikes and invasion.

- In an opinion article published in August 2002 in the magazine *[The Weekly Standard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Weekly_Standard)*, [Joshua Muravchik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua_Muravchik) of the [American Enterprise Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Enterprise_Institute),

  [^cite_note-344]

  &#x20;questioned Professor Herold's study entirely by one single incident that involved 25–93 deaths. He did not provide any estimate his own.

  [^cite_note-345]

- In a pair of January 2002 studies, Carl Conetta of the Project on Defense Alternatives estimates that "at least" 4,200–4,500 civilians were killed by mid-January 2002 as a result of the war and Coalition airstrikes, both directly as casualties of the aerial bombing campaign, and indirectly in the resulting humanitarian crisis.

- His first study, "Operation Enduring Freedom: Why a Higher Rate of Civilian Bombing Casualties?",

  [^cite_note-346]

  &#x20;released 18 January 2002, estimates that, at the low end, "at least" 1,000–1,300 civilians were directly killed in the aerial bombing campaign in just the three months between 7 October 2001 and 1 January 2002. The author found it impossible to provide an upper-end estimate of direct civilian casualties from the [Operation Enduring Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Enduring_Freedom) bombing campaign, which he noted had an increased use of [cluster bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cluster_bombs).

  [^cite_note-347]

  &#x20;In this lower-end estimate, only Western press sources were used for hard numbers, while heavy "reduction factors" were applied to Afghan government reports so that their estimates were reduced by as much as 75%.

  [^cite_note-348]

- In his companion study, "Strange Victory: A critical appraisal of Operation Enduring Freedom and the Afghanistan war",

  [^cite_note-349]

  &#x20;released 30 January 2002, Conetta estimates that "at least" 3,200 more Afghans died by mid-January 2002, of "starvation, exposure, associated illnesses, or injury sustained while in flight from war zones", as a result of the war and Coalition airstrikes.

- In similar numbers, a [Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times) review of US, British, and Pakistani newspapers and international wire services found that those news organizations reported between 1,067 and 1,201 direct civilian deaths during the five months from 7 October 2001 to 28 February 2002. This review excluded all civilian deaths in Afghanistan that did not get reported by US, British, or Pakistani news, excluded 497 deaths that did get reported in US, British, and Pakistani news but that were not specifically identified as civilian or military, and excluded 754 civilian deaths that the Taliban reported but were not independently confirmed.

  [^cite_note-350]

- According to [Jonathan Steele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Steele_\(journalist\)) of *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)*, between 20,000 and 49,600 people may have died of the consequences of the invasion by the spring of 2002.

  [^cite_note-351]

- 2,046 US military dead (339 non-hostile deaths), and 18,201 wounded in action.

  [^cite_note-Casualty_Data_USDOD-352]

- A report titled *Body Count* put together by [Physicians for Social Responsibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physicians_for_Social_Responsibility), [Physicians for Global Survival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physicians_for_Global_Survival), and [International Physicians for the Prevention of Nuclear War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Physicians_for_the_Prevention_of_Nuclear_War) (IPPNW) concluded that between 185,000 and 249,000 people had been killed as a result of the fighting in Afghanistan.

  [^cite_note-Casualties-341]

### Pakistan

Main articles: [American drone strikes in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_drone_strikes_in_Pakistan) and [Terrorism in Pakistan](2816470.md)

1,467 and 2,334 people were killed in US drone attacks as of 6 May 2011. Terrorist attacks have killed tens of thousands, and millions have been displaced.

The [War in Northwest Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Northwest_Pakistan) resulted in the deaths of 28,900+ militants from 2004 to 2018, with an unknown number captured, per the Pakistani government.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354] The majority of these were killed in engagements with the [Pakistan Armed Forces](23369.md). However, thousands were also killed in American [drone strikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drone_strikes_in_Pakistan).

### Somalia

There have been 7,000+ casualties in Somalia.

The December 2006 to January 2009 [Ethiopian-led intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somalia_War_\(2006%E2%80%932009\)) in Somalia resulted in the deaths of 6,000 to 8,000 Islamist insurgents, according to the Ethiopian government.[^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356] The Kenyan Defence Forces claimed another 700+ insurgents killed in their own intervention of October 2011 to May 2012.[^cite_note-357] American drone strikes, air strikes, and special forces ground raids in Somalia killed between 1,220 and 1,366 militants up to July 2019, according to the New American Foundation.[^cite_note-358]

- In December 2007, the Elman Peace and Human Rights Organization said it had verified 6,500 civilian deaths, 8,516 people wounded, and 1.5 million displaced from homes in Mogadishu alone during the year 2007.

  [^cite_note-359]

### Yemen

American forces (mostly via drone strikes) killed between 846 and 1,609 militants in Yemen (mostly [AQAP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Arabian_Peninsula) members) up to June 2019, according to a variety of media organizations, including the Bureau of Investigative Journalism and the New America Foundation.[^cite_note-360] An [Emirati](69328.md) spokesman for the [Saudi-led coalition intervening in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian-led_intervention_in_Yemen) claimed that they had killed 1,000 al-Qaeda linked militants and captured 1,500 up to August 2018.[^cite_note-361]

### Philippines and North Caucasus

Over 1,600 Islamic State fighters ([Abu Sayyaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Sayyaf) having sworn allegiance to ISIL in 2014) were killed by government forces in the Philippines from 2014 to 2017 alone.[^cite_note-362]

From April 2009 to March 2019, Russian military and police (primarily in the [North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasus)) killed 2,329 and captured 2,744 insurgents of the [Caucasus Emirate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Emirate) and related groups.[^cite_note-363]

### Total civilian estimates



Children wounded by American airstrikes in Afghanistan's [Surkh-Rōd District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surkh-R%C5%8Dd_District) in 2001

Between 363,939 and 387,072 civilians were killed in post–9/11 wars in Afghanistan, Pakistan, Iraq, Syria, Yemen, and other war zones, according to a 2021 report by the [Costs of War Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costs_of_War_Project) at Brown University's [Watson Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watson_Institute_for_International_and_Public_Affairs). Many more may have died due to related effects, including water loss and disease.[^cite_note-costs2021-31]

## Costs

The war on terror, spanning decades, is a multitrillion-dollar war that has cost much more than originally estimated.

According to the [Costs of War Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costs_of_War_Project) at Brown University's Watson Institute, the war on terror will have cost \$8 trillion for operations between 2001 and 2022 plus \$2.2 trillion in future costs of veterans' care over the next 30 years.[^cite_note-374] Out of this number, \$2.313 trillion is for Afghanistan, \$2.058 trillion for Iraq and Syria, and \$355 billion was spent on other warzones. The remainder was for DHS (\$1.1 trillion).

According to the Soufan Group in July 2015, the US government was spending \$9.4 million per day in operations against ISIL in Syria and Iraq.[^cite_note-375]

A March 2011 Congressional report[^cite_note-Daniel_Trotta-376] estimated war spending through the fiscal year 2011 at \$1.2 trillion, and future spending through 2021 (assuming a reduction to 45,000 troops) at \$1.8 trillion. A June 2011 academic report[^cite_note-Daniel_Trotta-376] covering additional areas of war spending estimated it through 2011 at \$2.7 trillion, and long-term spending at \$5.4 trillion including interest.[^cite_note-377]

In direct spending, the [United States Department of Defense](7279897.md) reports spending \$1.547 trillion from 2001 to February 2020 in war costs in Iraq, Syria, and Afghanistan.[^cite_note-378]

Adversary groups have taken an interest in [agricultural bioterrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_bioterrorism), and this is a continuing concern as of 2022.[^cite_note-addressing-379] The US government takes steps to prepare for threats from agricultural pathogens.[^cite_note-addressing-379] The [National Plant Disease Diagnostic Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Plant_Disease_Diagnostic_Network) (NPDN) coordinates efforts to combat agrowarfare against the US.[^cite_note-addressing-379]

## Criticism

Main article: [Criticism of the war on terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_war_on_terror)



Participants in a rally, dressed as hooded detainees

Criticism of the war on terror addresses issues of morality, efficiency, economics, and other related questions. Criticism has been directed at the phrase itself, calling it a [misnomer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misnomer). The notion of a "war" against "terrorism" has proven highly contentious, with critics charging that it has been exploited by participating governments to pursue long-standing policy/military objectives, including structural Islamophobia,[^cite_note-Monbiot2003-384][^cite_note-385] reduce [civil liberties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_liberties),[^cite_note-Wired-FBI-386] and infringe upon human rights. It is argued that the term war is not appropriate in this context (as in the "[war on drugs](1181646.md)") since there is no identifiable enemy, and that it is unlikely international terrorism can be brought to an end by military means.[^cite_note-Richissin-48]

Other critics, such as [Francis Fukuyama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Fukuyama), state that "[terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Definition_of_terrorism)" is not an enemy but a tactic, and calling it a "war on terror" obscures differences between conflicts such as anti-occupation [insurgents](589841.md) and international [mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen). With a military presence in [Iraq](7515928.md) and [Afghanistan](737.md) and its associated collateral damage, [Shirley Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirley_Williams) posits that this increases resentment and terrorist threats against the West.[^cite_note-387] There is also perceived US hypocrisy,[^cite_note-odom_terrorismtactic-388][^cite_note-389] media-induced hysteria,[^cite_note-390][^cite_note-391] and that differences in foreign and security policy have [damaged America's reputation internationally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_United_States_foreign_policy).[^cite_note-PEW-2007-392] The campaign has also been rebuked for being a perpetual war with no end-goal and for normalising permanent violence as the status quo.[^cite_note-393]

In addition, Professor Richard Jackson notes how countries like Russia, India, Israel, and China adopted the language of the war on terror to describe their own fight against domestic insurgents and dissidents. He argues that "Linking rebels and dissidents at home to the global 'war on terrorism' gives these governments both the freedom to crack down on them without fear of international condemnation, and in some cases, direct military assistance from America".[^cite_note-394]

Professor of Law [Antony Anghie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Anghie) has asserted that "war on terror" is essentially an [imperialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism) project that constitutes a breach of [International law](8195726.md) and the United Nations Charter.[^cite_note-Anghie2005-166] There has also been systematic cover-ups of [war crimes](48626.md) by military officers participating in campaign operations across the world. A public enquiry in the UK published in July 2023 reported that 3 British [SAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Air_Service) units were involved in the [summary executions](1227309.md) of at least 80 civilians during 2010–2013, accompanied by a decades-long cover-up at the highest echelons of [British special forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_special_forces).[^cite_note-395][^cite_note-396]

US occupations in Afghanistan and Iraq became heavily unpopular among the American public by the late 2000s.[^cite_note-Ackerman-34][^cite_note-Doherty,_Kiley-35] Numerous [US military](32212.md) veterans have handed back their service medals—including the [Global War on Terrorism Service Medal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_War_on_Terrorism_Service_Medal)—in fierce protest rallies denouncing the wars in Afghanistan and Iraq, with many condemning the military campaigns as "illegal occupation" of other countries.[^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398]

[Criticism of the war on terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_war_on_terror) has focused on its morality, efficiency, and cost. The notion of a "war on terror" remain contentious, with critics charging that it has been used to reduce [civil liberties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_liberties) and infringe upon human rights,[^cite_note-wired.com-36] such as controversial actions by the US including surveillance, torture, and [extraordinary rendition](1031191.md), and drone strikes that resulted in the deaths of alleged terrorists but also civilians.[^cite_note-Ball-39][^cite_note-theguardian.com-40][^cite_note-Drone_Warfare-41] Many of the US actions were supported by other countries, including the 54 countries that were involved with [CIA black sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_black_sites),[^cite_note-399] or those that assisted with drone strikes.[^cite_note-400]

### Forced displacement

According to a 2021 study by the [Costs of War Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watson_Institute_for_International_and_Public_Affairs), the several post-9/11 wars participated in by the United States in its war against terror have caused the displacement, conservatively calculated, of 38 million people in Afghanistan, Iraq, Libya, Syria, Yemen, Somalia, Philippines and Pakistan; 11.3 million remain displaced. This makes it the war that has caused the largest number of [forced displacements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_displacements) since 1900, except for [World War II](32927.md).[^cite_note-costs_displaced-30] Another report by the [Costs of War Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costs_of_War_Project) in 2023 estimates that the wars in these countries have caused a total number of 4.5–4.6 million deaths, including 3.6 million indirect deaths and 906,000–937,000 direct killings. Rather than basing this estimate on detailed data of the concerned countries, the Cost of War report multiplied the tallied violent death toll of 906,000–937,000 by four, "by applying the Geneva Declaration Secretariat's average ratio of four indirect for every one direct death."[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402] Of the approximately 925,000 violent deaths estimated by the Cost of War project, 542,000 were combatants, and 387,000 were civilians.[^cite_note-403] The war costs have risen over \$8 trillion for the [US Treasury](53667.md).[^cite_note-404] Critics accuse participating governments of using the "war on terror" to repress minorities or sideline domestic opponents,[^cite_note-Assimilation-42][^cite_note-911legacies.com-43] of fomenting [Islamophobia](162173.md) globally,[^cite_note-reflection_journal-44] and have criticized negative impacts to health and the environment resulting from it.[^cite_note-merip.org-45][^cite_note-Administration-46][^cite_note-Ameem_Lutfi-47] Critics assert that the term "war" is not appropriate in this context (much like the term "[war on drugs](1181646.md)") since terror is not an identifiable enemy; it is unlikely that international terrorism can be brought to an end by military means.[^cite_note-Richissin-48]

[^cite_note-12]: &#x20;The [Costs of War Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costs_of_War_Project) report defined post-9/11 war zones as conflicts that included significant United States counter-terrorism operations since 9/11, which in addition to the wars in [Iraq](55579888.md), [Afghanistan](19666611.md) and [Pakistan](12336420.md), also includes the civil wars in [Syria](30741795.md), [Yemen](46215853.md), [Libya](44791215.md) and [Somalia](3070332.md). The report derived their estimate of indirect deaths using a calculation from the [Geneva Declaration of Secretariat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Declaration_on_Armed_Violence_and_Development) which estimates that for every person directly killed by war, four more die from the indirect consequences of war. The report's author Stephanie Savell stated that in an ideal scenario, the preferable way of quantifying the total death toll would have been by studying excess mortality, or by using on-the-ground researchers in the affected countries.<sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-washingtonpost20230515-11)</sup>

[^cite_note-14]: &#x20;The definition of "indirect" is paraphrased by the Washington Post as "caused by the deterioration of economic, environmental, psychological and health conditions". Savell says it includes "mounting poverty, food insecurity, environmental contamination, the ongoing trauma of violence, and the destruction of health and public infrastructure, along with private property and means of livelihood".<sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-washingtonpost20230515-11)</sup>

[^cite_note-377]: &#x20;Among costs not covered by these figures are off-[DoD](7279897.md) spending beyond 2012, economic opportunity costs, state and local expenses not reimbursed by the federal government, nor reimbursements made to foreign coalition allies for their expenses.

Iraq is now the central front for the war on terror. While the United States and its partners have defeated Saddam Hussein's regime of terror, enemies of freedom—both members of the old regime and foreign terrorists who have come to Iraq—are making a desperate stance to reclaim this liberated nation for tyranny. We are resolved to win the global war on terrorism.
